Consulting English

A consulting English curriculum for discovery, hypotheses, stakeholder management, scope, executive recommendations, slide narratives, implementation risk, and client pushback.

For management consultants, strategy consultants, implementation consultants, analysts, engagement managers, client partners, internal consultants, and advisory teams.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Turn a request for benchmarking into an agreed decision question with boundaries, criteria, and an owner.
  • Use a working hypothesis to organize analysis while checking alternative explanations, overlapping categories, and evidence that could contradict it.
  • Prioritize a client data request, define the required measures, and agree a feasible delivery sequence without collecting unnecessary personal information.
  • Convert an analytical presentation into a supported recommendation with a specific request, visible limits, and an agreed test.
  • Respond constructively to additional work by distinguishing the agreed scope from the proposed change and explaining options without premature commitments.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All clients, engagements, people, figures, contracts, policies, and decisions are fictional. This is professional English practice, not legal, financial, statistical, or implementation advice. 01Client Discovery and Problem DefinitionThe requested study is not the decision+

Your goal

Turn a request for benchmarking into an agreed decision question with boundaries, criteria, and an owner.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The requested study is not the decision

Client sponsor Rita asks consultant Owen for a benchmark of support-center costs. During discovery, she explains that the executive committee must decide by 30 November whether to centralize the North and South support centers or retain them separately. The comparison must address total operating cost, response time, and service continuity, including transition effects. Staffing and customer-impact data are not yet available. Centralization has not been approved. Owen must define the decision and evidence needs before treating a peer cost comparison as a recommendation to close either site.

Rita: Client sponsor / Owen: Engagement lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What must the executive committee decide?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether to centralize the two centers or retain them separately

The stated choice is centralization versus separate operation; no closure or staffing decision has been approved.

2. When is the decision needed?

Answer and explanation

A. By 30 November

The brief gives 30 November as the executive committee's decision deadline.

3. Which criteria belong in the comparison?

Answer and explanation

A. Cost, response time, and service continuity, including transition effects

The sponsor explicitly requires three criteria and transition effects, not a cost-only benchmark.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A benchmark compares selected measures against a reference group. It does not itself decide the operating model. Different service hours, case complexity, or cost definitions can make apparently similar figures misleading unless the comparison is adjusted or qualified.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
discovery
Early inquiry to understand the client situation and decision.conduct client discovery
problem statement
A precise description of the question or issue to address.refine the problem statement
decision question
The choice that the analysis is intended to support.define the decision question
client sponsor
The client representative supporting and directing the engagement within their role.align with the client sponsor
decision owner
The person or body authorized to make the relevant choice.identify the decision owner
benchmark
A reference used for a defined comparison.establish a benchmark
peer group
Organizations or units selected for comparison.define the peer group
comparability
The degree to which a comparison uses sufficiently similar definitions and conditions.assess comparability
operating model
How an organization arranges people, processes, systems, and responsibilities.compare operating models
centralization
Concentrating specified activities or authority in a common location or structure.evaluate centralization
decentralization
Distributing specified activities or authority across units.assess decentralization
baseline
The defined starting position used for comparison.establish the baseline
decision criterion
A factor used to evaluate alternatives.agree decision criteria
constraint
A limit that an option must respect or explicitly address.surface a constraint
service continuity
Maintaining the required service through an operating change.assess service continuity
transition cost
A cost associated with moving from one arrangement to another.estimate transition costs
steady-state cost
Cost after a defined transition has settled.compare steady-state costs
response time
Elapsed time to provide a specified response.define response time
stakeholder map
A representation of relevant parties and their interests or roles.build a stakeholder map
in scope
Included in the agreed work boundaries.confirm what is in scope
out-of-scope
Excluded from the agreed work boundaries.identify out-of-scope work
success measure
A defined indicator of whether the intended result has been achieved.agree success measures
engagement charter
A document summarizing purpose, boundaries, roles, and arrangements.confirm the engagement charter
decision deadline
The date or time by which a choice is needed.work to the decision deadline

Separate steady-state cost from transition cost and disruption. A centralized model might look attractive after implementation while creating an unacceptable transition problem. The exercise does not establish which option is superior; it establishes the question the engagement must answer.

C Notice the language

Clarification can be a form of service

Questioning a requested deliverable can sound obstructive if it is detached from the client objective. Explain how the clarification makes the work more useful, then confirm the decision in the client language. Avoid implying that the sponsor asked a foolish question.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the purpose
What decision should this benchmark help you make?
State the real choice
The choice is centralization or continued separate operation.
Confirm the owner
The executive committee owns that decision.
Confirm the date
The recommendation needs to support the thirtieth-of-November decision.
Keep the option open
Centralization has not been approved.
Identify the criteria
We need to compare cost, response time, and service continuity.
Separate cost types
Show transition costs separately from steady-state costs.
Check comparability
Are the peer figures based on comparable services and definitions?
Name an evidence gap
We do not yet have the staffing and customer-impact data.
Avoid a premature conclusion
A lower peer cost would not by itself justify closing a site.
Set the boundary
This phase evaluates options; it does not authorize implementation.
Ask about constraints
Which service requirements must remain intact during transition?
Reflect the request
The benchmark is one input to the operating-model decision.
Confirm the deliverable
We will produce a decision-focused option comparison.
Check understanding
Is that the choice you need the analysis to support?
Close discovery
Let us record the question, criteria, owner, deadline, and evidence needs.
6 language and precision notes
Should help
Connects the requested analysis to its intended purpose.
The choice is
States alternatives without selecting one prematurely.
Has not been approved
Prevents exploration becoming an implied commitment.
By itself
Limits what one piece of evidence can establish.
One input
Positions benchmarking within a wider decision.
Let us record
Turns a verbal clarification into a shared reference.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which problem statement fits the brief?

Answer and explanation

C. Should North and South be centralized, given cost, response time, continuity, and transition effects?

The appropriate question preserves the actual alternatives and criteria without presuming an approval or result.

2. Which benchmark caveat matters?

Answer and explanation

B. Peer service scope and cost definitions may differ.

A useful peer comparison requires comparable boundaries and measures. Size, a common ratio, and a shared year do not by themselves align scope, complexity, or service hours.

3. What does the evidence gap mean?

Answer and explanation

B. Staffing and customer-impact information is still needed.

The gap identifies information required for the comparison without deciding the eventual recommendation.

4. Which phrase preserves engagement authority?

Answer and explanation

A. This phase evaluates options; implementation requires a separate authorized decision.

An analytical engagement supports the client's decision but does not itself authorize implementation.

E Conversations

The requested study is not the decision

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rita: Client sponsor / Owen: Engagement lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • transition cost
  • comparability
  • decision deadline
  • peer group
  • decision question
  • in scope
  • baseline
  • engagement charter
  • decision criteria
  • decision owner
  1. Rita
    The committee keeps asking why our support centers cost so much. Could you give us a peer benchmark before the next decision meeting?
  2. Owen
    Before we define the 1, what decision should the benchmark help you make? That will determine which comparisons are useful and which would simply add more numbers.
  3. Rita
    We need to decide whether North and South should become one centralized support operation or continue running separately.
  4. Owen
    Then our 2 is about the operating model. The cost benchmark is an input, but we should not let it silently become a recommendation to close either site.
  5. Rita
    Agreed. Nothing has been approved. The executive committee needs a recommendation by the thirtieth of November.
  6. Owen
    I will record that 3. We should also confirm who will supply information and when the committee needs material for its own review before that date.
  7. Rita
    Cost matters, but we also need acceptable response times and continuity. A cheap arrangement that interrupts service would not solve the problem.
  8. Owen
    Those are our 4. We need definitions for each, so the team does not optimize a cost measure while overlooking the service requirements the committee actually values.
  9. Rita
    I can imagine a lower annual cost after centralizing. Getting there is another matter. We have not costed the move or the service disruption.
  10. Owen
    Separate 5 from the steady-state comparison. We should show temporary effects and ongoing costs distinctly, rather than present a future operating estimate as the whole business case.
  11. Rita
    The peer organizations may handle different types of support requests. Some provide fewer service hours than we do.
  12. Owen
    That affects 6. We need matching definitions or visible qualifications. A lower cost elsewhere does not tell us that either of your centers is inefficient under the same conditions.
  13. Rita
    I can arrange access to the current service figures. Staffing and customer-impact information will take longer to obtain.
  14. Owen
    Let us establish the 7 and list those gaps. We can plan the evidence work without filling missing figures with assumptions that look like confirmed client data.
  15. Rita
    Should the initial work include a detailed implementation plan, or would that get ahead of the decision?
  16. Owen
    We should define what is 8. The option comparison needs transition implications, but a full implementation plan is a separate level of work that we should not assume is included.
  17. Rita
    The committee will make the choice. I can sponsor the engagement and coordinate the inputs, but I cannot approve a closure myself.
  18. Owen
    That clarifies the 9. We can direct the recommendation to the committee and ask you to confirm the factual inputs and engagement arrangements within your role.
  19. Rita
    Please summarize the question, boundaries, criteria, and dates so that everyone starts with the same understanding.
  20. Owen
    I will put them in the 10. Then the benchmark serves the actual choice, and the team has a clear basis for deciding what evidence to collect first.

Answers and explanations
  1. peer group Turn 2. The comparison organizations should fit the decision and relevant service characteristics, not merely be familiar names.
  2. decision question Turn 4. The actual choice is centralization versus separate operation; benchmarking supports that decision rather than replacing it.
  3. decision deadline Turn 6. The committee needs the recommendation by 30 November, which guides evidence collection and review timing.
  4. decision criteria Turn 8. Cost, response time, and continuity are the agreed comparison factors, including effects during transition.
  5. transition cost Turn 10. These costs arise during the change and must remain distinct from settled operating costs.
  6. comparability Turn 12. Different hours, case types, or cost definitions can distort an apparently simple peer comparison.
  7. baseline Turn 14. The baseline describes current operations; missing staffing and customer information must remain explicit.
  8. in scope Turn 16. The agreed analysis includes transition implications but does not automatically include a full implementation plan.
  9. decision owner Turn 18. The executive committee makes the choice; the sponsor coordinates without independently authorizing closure.
  10. engagement charter Turn 20. The charter records the shared purpose, boundaries, roles, and timing before detailed analysis begins.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rita: The committee keeps asking why our support centers cost so much. Could you give us a peer benchmark before the next decision meeting?
  2. Owen: Before we define the peer group, what decision should the benchmark help you make? That will determine which comparisons are useful and which would simply add more numbers.
  3. Rita: We need to decide whether North and South should become one centralized support operation or continue running separately.
  4. Owen: Then our decision question is about the operating model. The cost benchmark is an input, but we should not let it silently become a recommendation to close either site.
  5. Rita: Agreed. Nothing has been approved. The executive committee needs a recommendation by the thirtieth of November.
  6. Owen: I will record that decision deadline. We should also confirm who will supply information and when the committee needs material for its own review before that date.
  7. Rita: Cost matters, but we also need acceptable response times and continuity. A cheap arrangement that interrupts service would not solve the problem.
  8. Owen: Those are our decision criteria. We need definitions for each, so the team does not optimize a cost measure while overlooking the service requirements the committee actually values.
  9. Rita: I can imagine a lower annual cost after centralizing. Getting there is another matter. We have not costed the move or the service disruption.
  10. Owen: Separate transition cost from the steady-state comparison. We should show temporary effects and ongoing costs distinctly, rather than present a future operating estimate as the whole business case.
  11. Rita: The peer organizations may handle different types of support requests. Some provide fewer service hours than we do.
  12. Owen: That affects comparability. We need matching definitions or visible qualifications. A lower cost elsewhere does not tell us that either of your centers is inefficient under the same conditions.
  13. Rita: I can arrange access to the current service figures. Staffing and customer-impact information will take longer to obtain.
  14. Owen: Let us establish the baseline and list those gaps. We can plan the evidence work without filling missing figures with assumptions that look like confirmed client data.
  15. Rita: Should the initial work include a detailed implementation plan, or would that get ahead of the decision?
  16. Owen: We should define what is in scope. The option comparison needs transition implications, but a full implementation plan is a separate level of work that we should not assume is included.
  17. Rita: The committee will make the choice. I can sponsor the engagement and coordinate the inputs, but I cannot approve a closure myself.
  18. Owen: That clarifies the decision owner. We can direct the recommendation to the committee and ask you to confirm the factual inputs and engagement arrangements within your role.
  19. Rita: Please summarize the question, boundaries, criteria, and dates so that everyone starts with the same understanding.
  20. Owen: I will put them in the engagement charter. Then the benchmark serves the actual choice, and the team has a clear basis for deciding what evidence to collect first.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Benchmarking for which decision?

The lesson's client asks for benchmarking but needs to decide whether to centralize a service.

  1. Engagement manager: Before we scope the benchmarking study, could you clarify which decision the findings need to support?
  2. Client service director: We need to decide whether to centralize the service. Benchmarking is the approach we initially had in mind.
  3. Engagement manager: Then the decision question concerns centralization, rather than simply how we compare with other organizations.
  4. Client service director: Yes. Would you still use benchmarking, or are you suggesting we abandon the request?
  5. Engagement manager: It may contribute evidence, but discovery should first establish the problem and the criteria for that decision.
  6. Client service director: What do you need clarified before proposing the study's scope and stakeholder discussions?
  7. Engagement manager: Which service is in scope, what problem centralization should address, and who will make the decision.
  8. Client service director: Those need explicit answers. We shouldn't assume our initial request contains an agreed problem statement.
  9. Engagement manager: Exactly. I'll frame those questions for our next discussion without treating centralization as the preferred outcome.
  10. Client service director: Good. Let's define the decision before committing to a research method or a recommendation.
A dashboard request conceals a handover problem

Separate fictional case: a client asks for a new dashboard because orders repeatedly wait between sales and operations; the reason for the waiting is unknown.

  1. Discovery consultant: You requested a dashboard because orders wait between sales and operations. What decision should it support?
  2. Client operations lead: We want fewer stalled orders, but we haven't specified who would act on the information.
  3. Discovery consultant: Could we clarify the handover problem before assuming a new display is the required solution?
  4. Client operations lead: Yes. We know orders wait, but we don't yet know whether missing information or unclear ownership causes it.
  5. Discovery consultant: Which stakeholders can explain what they receive, what they need, and when they accept responsibility?
  6. Client operations lead: Sales and operations both need to contribute. I can arrange a joint discovery conversation.
  7. Discovery consultant: Good. We should not write a problem statement that already assumes the dashboard will solve the issue.
  8. Client operations lead: Then the initial question is how to reduce waiting at the handover, with the solution still open.
  9. Discovery consultant: Exactly. We'll clarify the workflow and desired result before recommending a particular tool.
  10. Client operations lead: I'll invite the relevant owners and make clear that we're defining the problem, not approving a dashboard build.
Cost reduction or more reliable delivery?

Separate fictional case: a sponsor asks for efficiency improvements, while a project brief mentions both lower cost and more reliable delivery without ranking them.

  1. Consulting lead: The brief uses efficiency, but lists lower cost and more reliable delivery. Which objective drives the decision?
  2. Client sponsor: We haven't ranked them. Different stakeholders may be using efficiency to mean different things.
  3. Consulting lead: Could we clarify what tradeoff you would consider acceptable before we define the problem statement?
  4. Client sponsor: Yes. I don't want the team to recommend a cost reduction that silently weakens delivery reliability.
  5. Consulting lead: Then both objectives need explicit definitions, and we should identify who can resolve a conflict between them.
  6. Client sponsor: I can convene the relevant managers, but I should not assume they all share my priorities.
  7. Consulting lead: Good. Discovery should surface those differences rather than translate an ambiguous word into a single assumed target.
  8. Client sponsor: Would you ask each manager what decision they need to make and what evidence would help?
  9. Consulting lead: Exactly. We'll compare their answers and bring unresolved priorities back for an explicit discussion.
  10. Client sponsor: Let's do that before agreeing a scope that measures the wrong version of efficiency.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The requested study is not the decision
  1. Read Rita and Owen aloud, then swap roles. Stress the actual choice and the three comparison criteria.
  2. Use the answer key to correct all ten gaps. Reread the peer-comparison and transition-cost exchanges without dropping their qualifications.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify a different requested study

A sponsor requests a vendor-price comparison. The actual choice is whether to renew the current provider or run a new competition. The procurement committee decides by 15 January, using total cost, service performance, and transition risk.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Consultant: "The actual alternatives are renewal or ___."

Answer and explanation

D. a new competition

The price comparison supports this stated choice, not an already approved provider change.

2. Sponsor: "The decision owner is the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. procurement committee

The committee holds the stated decision role, while the sponsor supplies engagement direction.

3. Consultant: "The decision deadline is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 15 January

This date determines when the comparison must support the authorized choice.

4. Sponsor: "The comparison must include total cost, performance, and ___."

Answer and explanation

D. transition risk

Transition risk is an explicit criterion alongside cost and service performance.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Client Discovery and Problem Definition
Original fictional case: Client sponsor Rita asks consultant Owen for a benchmark of support-center costs. During discovery, she explains that the executive committee must decide by 30 November whether to centralize the North and South support centers or retain them separately. The comparison must address total operating cost, response time, and service continuity, including transition effects. Staffing and customer-impact data are not yet available. Centralization has not been approved. Owen must define the decision and evidence needs before treating a peer cost comparison as a recommendation to close either site.
Language workshop: The requested study is not the decision
Communication goal: Turn a request for benchmarking into an agreed decision question with boundaries, criteria, and an owner.
Language guidance: A benchmark compares selected measures against a reference group. It does not itself decide the operating model. Different service hours, case complexity, or cost definitions can make apparently similar figures misleading unless the comparison is adjusted or qualified.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What decision should this benchmark help you make? / The choice is centralization or continued separate operation. / The executive committee owns that decision. / The recommendation needs to support the thirtieth-of-November decision. / Centralization has not been approved. / We need to compare cost, response time, and service continuity. / Show transition costs separately from steady-state costs. / Are the peer figures based on comparable services and definitions? / We do not yet have the staffing and customer-impact data. / A lower peer cost would not by itself justify closing a site. / This phase evaluates options; it does not authorize implementation. / Which service requirements must remain intact during transition? / The benchmark is one input to the operating-model decision. / We will produce a decision-focused option comparison. / Is that the choice you need the analysis to support? / Let us record the question, criteria, owner, deadline, and evidence needs.
Vocabulary: discovery: Early inquiry to understand the client situation and decision. / problem statement: A precise description of the question or issue to address. / decision question: The choice that the analysis is intended to support. / client sponsor: The client representative supporting and directing the engagement within their role. / decision owner: The person or body authorized to make the relevant choice. / benchmark: A reference used for a defined comparison. / peer group: Organizations or units selected for comparison. / comparability: The degree to which a comparison uses sufficiently similar definitions and conditions. / operating model: How an organization arranges people, processes, systems, and responsibilities. / centralization: Concentrating specified activities or authority in a common location or structure. / decentralization: Distributing specified activities or authority across units. / baseline: The defined starting position used for comparison. / decision criterion: A factor used to evaluate alternatives. / constraint: A limit that an option must respect or explicitly address. / service continuity: Maintaining the required service through an operating change. / transition cost: A cost associated with moving from one arrangement to another. / steady-state cost: Cost after a defined transition has settled. / response time: Elapsed time to provide a specified response. / stakeholder map: A representation of relevant parties and their interests or roles. / in scope: Included in the agreed work boundaries. / out-of-scope: Excluded from the agreed work boundaries. / success measure: A defined indicator of whether the intended result has been achieved. / engagement charter: A document summarizing purpose, boundaries, roles, and arrangements. / decision deadline: The date or time by which a choice is needed.
Speaking task: Read Rita and Owen aloud, then swap roles. Stress the actual choice and the three comparison criteria. Use the answer key to correct all ten gaps. Reread the peer-comparison and transition-cost exchanges without dropping their qualifications. Deliver Owen's final engagement summary again, retaining the decision owner, deadline, evidence gaps, and boundary between analysis and implementation.
Partner: Rita: Client sponsor / Owen: Engagement lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A sponsor requests a vendor-price comparison. The actual choice is whether to renew the current provider or run a new competition. The procurement committee decides by 15 January, using total cost, service performance, and transition risk.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rita: The committee will make the choice. I can sponsor the engagement and coordinate the inputs, but I cannot approve a closure myself.
Owen: That clarifies the decision owner. We can direct the recommendation to the committee and ask you to confirm the factual inputs and engagement arrangements within your role.
Rita: Please summarize the question, boundaries, criteria, and dates so that everyone starts with the same understanding.
Owen: I will put them in the engagement charter. Then the benchmark serves the actual choice, and the team has a clear basis for deciding what evidence to collect first.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Client Discovery and Problem Definition
Original fictional case: Client sponsor Rita asks consultant Owen for a benchmark of support-center costs. During discovery, she explains that the executive committee must decide by 30 November whether to centralize the North and South support centers or retain them separately. The comparison must address total operating cost, response time, and service continuity, including transition effects. Staffing and customer-impact data are not yet available. Centralization has not been approved. Owen must define the decision and evidence needs before treating a peer cost comparison as a recommendation to close either site.
Language workshop: The requested study is not the decision
Communication goal: Turn a request for benchmarking into an agreed decision question with boundaries, criteria, and an owner.
Language guidance: A benchmark compares selected measures against a reference group. It does not itself decide the operating model. Different service hours, case complexity, or cost definitions can make apparently similar figures misleading unless the comparison is adjusted or qualified.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What decision should this benchmark help you make? / The choice is centralization or continued separate operation. / The executive committee owns that decision. / The recommendation needs to support the thirtieth-of-November decision. / Centralization has not been approved. / We need to compare cost, response time, and service continuity. / Show transition costs separately from steady-state costs. / Are the peer figures based on comparable services and definitions? / We do not yet have the staffing and customer-impact data. / A lower peer cost would not by itself justify closing a site. / This phase evaluates options; it does not authorize implementation. / Which service requirements must remain intact during transition? / The benchmark is one input to the operating-model decision. / We will produce a decision-focused option comparison. / Is that the choice you need the analysis to support? / Let us record the question, criteria, owner, deadline, and evidence needs.
Vocabulary: discovery: Early inquiry to understand the client situation and decision. / problem statement: A precise description of the question or issue to address. / decision question: The choice that the analysis is intended to support. / client sponsor: The client representative supporting and directing the engagement within their role. / decision owner: The person or body authorized to make the relevant choice. / benchmark: A reference used for a defined comparison. / peer group: Organizations or units selected for comparison. / comparability: The degree to which a comparison uses sufficiently similar definitions and conditions. / operating model: How an organization arranges people, processes, systems, and responsibilities. / centralization: Concentrating specified activities or authority in a common location or structure. / decentralization: Distributing specified activities or authority across units. / baseline: The defined starting position used for comparison. / decision criterion: A factor used to evaluate alternatives. / constraint: A limit that an option must respect or explicitly address. / service continuity: Maintaining the required service through an operating change. / transition cost: A cost associated with moving from one arrangement to another. / steady-state cost: Cost after a defined transition has settled. / response time: Elapsed time to provide a specified response. / stakeholder map: A representation of relevant parties and their interests or roles. / in scope: Included in the agreed work boundaries. / out-of-scope: Excluded from the agreed work boundaries. / success measure: A defined indicator of whether the intended result has been achieved. / engagement charter: A document summarizing purpose, boundaries, roles, and arrangements. / decision deadline: The date or time by which a choice is needed.
Speaking task: Read Rita and Owen aloud, then swap roles. Stress the actual choice and the three comparison criteria. Use the answer key to correct all ten gaps. Reread the peer-comparison and transition-cost exchanges without dropping their qualifications. Deliver Owen's final engagement summary again, retaining the decision owner, deadline, evidence gaps, and boundary between analysis and implementation.
Partner: Rita: Client sponsor / Owen: Engagement lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A sponsor requests a vendor-price comparison. The actual choice is whether to renew the current provider or run a new competition. The procurement committee decides by 15 January, using total cost, service performance, and transition risk.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rita: The committee will make the choice. I can sponsor the engagement and coordinate the inputs, but I cannot approve a closure myself.
Owen: That clarifies the decision owner. We can direct the recommendation to the committee and ask you to confirm the factual inputs and engagement arrangements within your role.
Rita: Please summarize the question, boundaries, criteria, and dates so that everyone starts with the same understanding.
Owen: I will put them in the engagement charter. Then the benchmark serves the actual choice, and the team has a clear basis for deciding what evidence to collect first.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Professional audience: management consultants, strategy consultants, implementation consultants, analysts, engagement managers, client partners, internal consultants, and advisory teams
Scope: All clients, engagements, people, figures, contracts, policies, and decisions are fictional. This is professional English practice, not legal, financial, statistical, or implementation advice. Real decisions require the applicable evidence, contractual terms, approvals, and qualified review. The cited organizations do not endorse this book.

02Hypotheses and Issue TreesA frequent complaint is not yet the cause of churn+

Your goal

Use a working hypothesis to organize analysis while checking alternative explanations, overlapping categories, and evidence that could contradict it.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A frequent complaint is not yet the cause of churn

A client lost 60 accounts last quarter. Twenty departing customers volunteered for interviews: 12 mentioned price, 9 mentioned service delays, and 5 mentioned competitor features. Each person could mention more than one issue. Consultant Ada wants to lead with price as the cause of churn; manager Karim asks for a testable hypothesis and a structured inquiry into the alternatives. The interview sample is not established as representative. Timing, customer segments, actual prices paid, and comparable nondeparting accounts have not yet been analyzed. No causal driver has been isolated.

Ada: Consultant / Karim: Engagement manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many departing accounts were interviewed?

Answer and explanation

B. Twenty of the sixty departing accounts

Twenty customers volunteered for interviews, while sixty accounts departed in total.

2. Why do the mentions add to more than twenty?

Answer and explanation

A. Respondents could mention multiple issues.

Multiple responses permit overlapping categories, so the mention counts need not sum to the number interviewed.

3. What has the team established about causation?

Answer and explanation

B. No causal driver has been isolated.

The brief explicitly leaves causal drivers unresolved despite the frequency of particular interview mentions.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twelve of twenty interviewees mentioned price: 60% of that interview group. It is not evidence that price caused 60% of all sixty departures. The price, service, and competitor counts overlap because respondents could give more than one answer.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
hypothesis
A proposed explanation that can be examined against evidence.test a hypothesis
issue tree
A structured breakdown of a question into related subquestions.build an issue tree
hypothesis tree
A breakdown of a proposed explanation into testable supporting claims.develop a hypothesis tree
workstream
A coordinated part of the engagement's work.assign a workstream
churn
Customer or account loss, defined over a specified period and population.analyze account churn
churn driver
A factor contributing to customer departure.investigate churn drivers
exit interview
A conversation with someone leaving a service or organization.conduct exit interviews
multi-response question
A question allowing more than one answer category.interpret a multi-response question
mention count
The number of recorded references to a specified topic.report mention counts
sample
The observations included in an analysis.define the sample
sampling bias
Distortion associated with how observations enter a sample.assess sampling bias
MECE
Mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive; a structuring aim of avoiding overlap and missing categories.test a MECE structure
overlap
Inclusion of the same observation in more than one category.identify category overlap
double counting
Counting the same item more than intended in a total.prevent double counting
alternative explanation
A different possible account of an observed result.test alternative explanations
disconfirming evidence
Information that weakens or contradicts a hypothesis.seek disconfirming evidence
confirmation bias
Favoring information that supports an existing belief.challenge confirmation bias
causal mechanism
The process through which one factor could produce an effect.specify the causal mechanism
segmentation
Dividing a population into defined groups for analysis.use customer segmentation
cohort
A group sharing a defined starting characteristic or period.compare customer cohorts
price sensitivity
Responsiveness to price changes under specified conditions.examine price sensitivity
confounder
Another factor that complicates a proposed causal interpretation.examine potential confounders
triangulation
Comparing different evidence sources or methods.triangulate the finding
analytical priority
A question selected for attention based on decision relevance and evidence needs.set analytical priorities

MECE is an organizing aim, not a label that makes real-world causes independent. A customer can experience both a price increase and a service failure. Define how observations are coded, and avoid forcing a multi-cause account into a false single-cause total.

C Notice the language

Challenge the logic, not the analyst

A sharp question about an explanation can improve the work without rejecting the person who proposed it. State which part is useful, then ask what would weaken it. A team that can revise its first answer is more credible than one that protects it from contrary evidence.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Keep the explanation provisional
Price is a working hypothesis, not an established churn driver.
State the sample
We interviewed twenty of the sixty departing accounts.
Limit the percentage
Twelve of those twenty interviewees mentioned price.
Explain overlap
Respondents could name more than one issue.
Avoid a false total
The categories are not mutually exclusive.
Ask for a mechanism
How would the proposed price effect lead to departure?
Test the alternative
We also need to examine service experience and competitor changes.
Look for contradiction
What evidence would weaken the price hypothesis?
Check timing
Did the price change precede the departure?
Compare carefully
How do otherwise comparable nondeparting accounts differ?
Segment the evidence
Separate the patterns by relevant customer group.
Check actual exposure
Use prices actually paid, not only the published list price.
Avoid a causal shortcut
A frequent complaint does not by itself isolate a cause.
Prioritize the work
Start with questions that could change the client decision.
Revise openly
The new evidence may require us to change the initial explanation.
Close the analysis plan
Assign a test, evidence source, and owner to each priority question.
6 language and precision notes
Working hypothesis
Gives the team a testable starting point rather than a fixed conclusion.
Those twenty
Keeps the denominator tied to the interview sample.
Could name more than one
Explains why categories overlap.
Would weaken
Actively invites evidence against the preferred explanation.
Actually paid
Distinguishes realized prices from advertised prices.
Could change the decision
Uses relevance rather than analytical convenience to set priorities.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which finding is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. Twelve of twenty volunteer interviewees mentioned price.

The supported statement retains the sample and what respondents said without converting mentions into causal proof.

2. Which follow-up best tests rather than protects the hypothesis?

Answer and explanation

D. Examine whether comparable accounts with similar price changes stayed.

Comparable retained accounts can challenge the explanation. Forced recoding changes the evidence; a different product line may be an unsuitable comparison; questioning only price complainants preserves selection bias.

3. How should the issue categories be presented?

Answer and explanation

C. As overlapping mentions, with the coding method explained

The multi-response design requires clear overlap disclosure to avoid misleading totals.

4. What makes an analytical priority useful?

Answer and explanation

C. It could change the decision and has an explicit evidence test.

Priority should follow decision relevance and a testable inquiry, not convenience or guaranteed agreement.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

A frequent complaint is not yet the cause of churn

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ada: Consultant / Karim: Engagement manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • disconfirming evidence
  • sample
  • hypothesis
  • analytical priority
  • issue tree
  • overlap
  • causal mechanism
  • segmentation
  • triangulation
  • MECE
  1. Ada
    Price tops the exit-interview list. My draft says it is driving churn. Before I send that to the client, does the evidence justify the wording?
  2. Karim
    Make it a 1, not a finding about cause. We have twenty volunteer interviews from sixty departures, and the way people entered that sample could affect what we hear.
  3. Ada
    Twelve mentioned price, nine mentioned service delays, and five mentioned competitor features. The total is greater than twenty because people could mention several issues.
  4. Karim
    Then disclose the 2. Those are topic mentions, not separate groups of departing accounts. A clean-looking chart must not imply that the categories are mutually exclusive when they are not.
  5. Ada
    Twelve out of twenty is sixty percent. I should say sixty percent of interviewees mentioned price, not sixty percent of departures were caused by price.
  6. Karim
    Exactly. The 3 defines that percentage. We have not established representativeness, and mentioning a concern does not isolate its causal effect on the decision to leave.
  7. Ada
    How should I organize the next analysis without creating a huge list of every possible reason someone might cancel?
  8. Karim
    Build an 4 around questions that could change the retention decision. Include price exposure, service experience, and competitor developments, while being clear that real causes may interact.
  9. Ada
    We sometimes call our trees MECE. That could be misleading if we use the label to suggest that these customer experiences cannot coexist.
  10. Karim
    Treat 5 as a structuring check, not a substitute for understanding the data. Define the coding and identify gaps or overlaps instead of forcing complex departures into artificial single-cause boxes.
  11. Ada
    For price, we could check the amounts customers actually paid and whether a change occurred before cancellation.
  12. Karim
    That begins to test a 6. Timing matters, and list prices may differ from actual exposure because of discounts or contract terms. We should not assume every account faced the same change.
  13. Ada
    What about accounts that had the same price increase and stayed? I have focused on departures, but that comparison could change the story.
  14. Karim
    That could provide 7. Check comparability and relevant differences before interpreting it. The aim is to test the explanation, not select the most convenient retained accounts to prove the opposite.
  15. Ada
    Service delays may affect some customer groups more than others. A combined result could hide that pattern.
  16. Karim
    Use relevant 8. But document how groups are defined, and do not repeatedly regroup the data just until a compelling story appears.
  17. Ada
    We can compare interview accounts, service records, and actual commercial terms to see where the evidence agrees or conflicts.
  18. Karim
    That is useful 9. Keep discrepancies visible. Agreement across sources may strengthen an interpretation, but it does not automatically remove all alternative explanations.
  19. Ada
    I will revise the update to state the interview pattern and the tests needed before recommending a price response.
  20. Karim
    Good. Set each 10 with an evidence source and owner. The initial hypothesis should help us learn faster, not make the team resistant to what the next analysis finds.

Answers and explanations
  1. hypothesis Turn 2. Price is an explanation to test, not a cause established by interview frequency.
  2. overlap Turn 4. Several topics can describe the same respondent, so their counts can exceed the interview count.
  3. sample Turn 6. The twenty interviewees are not automatically representative of all sixty departures, and mentions do not establish cause.
  4. issue tree Turn 8. The tree organizes decision-relevant subquestions without treating its initial branches as proven explanations.
  5. MECE Turn 10. MECE checks structure; the label does not make interacting customer experiences independent or exclusive.
  6. causal mechanism Turn 12. This asks how and when price could influence departure, beyond merely recording a repeated complaint.
  7. disconfirming evidence Turn 14. Comparable retained accounts could weaken the price explanation; comparison quality still needs examination.
  8. segmentation Turn 16. Defined customer groups can reveal different patterns that a combined result might conceal.
  9. triangulation Turn 18. Comparing evidence sources can inform the explanation without automatically establishing a causal effect.
  10. analytical priority Turn 20. A priority is a decision-relevant test with an evidence source and owner, not merely an easy task.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ada: Price tops the exit-interview list. My draft says it is driving churn. Before I send that to the client, does the evidence justify the wording?
  2. Karim: Make it a hypothesis, not a finding about cause. We have twenty volunteer interviews from sixty departures, and the way people entered that sample could affect what we hear.
  3. Ada: Twelve mentioned price, nine mentioned service delays, and five mentioned competitor features. The total is greater than twenty because people could mention several issues.
  4. Karim: Then disclose the overlap. Those are topic mentions, not separate groups of departing accounts. A clean-looking chart must not imply that the categories are mutually exclusive when they are not.
  5. Ada: Twelve out of twenty is sixty percent. I should say sixty percent of interviewees mentioned price, not sixty percent of departures were caused by price.
  6. Karim: Exactly. The sample defines that percentage. We have not established representativeness, and mentioning a concern does not isolate its causal effect on the decision to leave.
  7. Ada: How should I organize the next analysis without creating a huge list of every possible reason someone might cancel?
  8. Karim: Build an issue tree around questions that could change the retention decision. Include price exposure, service experience, and competitor developments, while being clear that real causes may interact.
  9. Ada: We sometimes call our trees MECE. That could be misleading if we use the label to suggest that these customer experiences cannot coexist.
  10. Karim: Treat MECE as a structuring check, not a substitute for understanding the data. Define the coding and identify gaps or overlaps instead of forcing complex departures into artificial single-cause boxes.
  11. Ada: For price, we could check the amounts customers actually paid and whether a change occurred before cancellation.
  12. Karim: That begins to test a causal mechanism. Timing matters, and list prices may differ from actual exposure because of discounts or contract terms. We should not assume every account faced the same change.
  13. Ada: What about accounts that had the same price increase and stayed? I have focused on departures, but that comparison could change the story.
  14. Karim: That could provide disconfirming evidence. Check comparability and relevant differences before interpreting it. The aim is to test the explanation, not select the most convenient retained accounts to prove the opposite.
  15. Ada: Service delays may affect some customer groups more than others. A combined result could hide that pattern.
  16. Karim: Use relevant segmentation. But document how groups are defined, and do not repeatedly regroup the data just until a compelling story appears.
  17. Ada: We can compare interview accounts, service records, and actual commercial terms to see where the evidence agrees or conflicts.
  18. Karim: That is useful triangulation. Keep discrepancies visible. Agreement across sources may strengthen an interpretation, but it does not automatically remove all alternative explanations.
  19. Ada: I will revise the update to state the interview pattern and the tests needed before recommending a price response.
  20. Karim: Good. Set each analytical priority with an evidence source and owner. The initial hypothesis should help us learn faster, not make the team resistant to what the next analysis finds.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Price is a hypothesis, not the whole issue tree

The lesson's team attributes churn to price without examining service experience or competitor changes.

  1. Workstream lead: The team thinks price is driving churn, but we haven't examined service experience or competitor changes.
  2. Engagement manager: Then price belongs in the issue tree as a hypothesis, not as an established conclusion.
  3. Workstream lead: Could we organize the evidence plan around price first because that explanation feels most plausible?
  4. Engagement manager: We can prioritize questions, but the plan must still test alternatives rather than only seek supporting examples.
  5. Workstream lead: I'll separate the price, service-experience, and competitor questions so the workstream doesn't overlook them.
  6. Engagement manager: Good. What will you say if the client asks whether we have already found the churn cause?
  7. Workstream lead: We have a price hypothesis, while other explanations remain unexamined. Attribution is not established.
  8. Engagement manager: Exactly. Avoid making the wording more certain simply because the slide needs a short title.
  9. Workstream lead: I'll show what evidence each branch requires and flag any data we cannot obtain.
  10. Engagement manager: Then our next discussion can compare findings across explanations rather than defend the first idea.
Late deliveries and a recent staffing change

Separate fictional case: delivery delays rose after a staffing change; order mix and supplier performance have not been examined.

  1. Operations consultant: Delays rose after the staffing change. That timing suggests a question, but doesn't establish the cause.
  2. Client project lead: Our managers already blame the new staffing model. How should the analysis respond to their view?
  3. Operations consultant: Treat it as a hypothesis and examine alternative explanations, including order mix and supplier performance.
  4. Client project lead: Would that look like avoiding the staffing issue rather than testing it directly?
  5. Operations consultant: No. A balanced evidence plan tests the staffing explanation alongside other plausible contributors.
  6. Client project lead: Then the issue tree should distinguish observed delay growth from the possible reasons beneath it.
  7. Operations consultant: Exactly. We should also define the period and measure consistently before comparing results.
  8. Client project lead: I'll ask the managers for their supporting observations without recording their belief as a verified finding.
  9. Operations consultant: Good. Each workstream can report evidence, gaps, and contradictory examples rather than only confirm expectations.
  10. Client project lead: Let's bring that structure to the next meeting and keep the causal conclusion open.
One interview does not close a branch

Separate fictional case: one manager says customer onboarding delays never involve approvals; the team has not reviewed approval records or other interviews.

  1. Analyst: One manager said approvals never delay onboarding. Should I remove that branch from the issue tree?
  2. Case team leader: Not on one interview alone. It is a stakeholder account, not a complete test of the hypothesis.
  3. Analyst: Then I should record the statement and identify what approval evidence would help assess it.
  4. Case team leader: Yes. We need relevant records and other perspectives before treating the branch as ruled out.
  5. Analyst: Could the manager's account still guide which cases we examine first, without deciding the answer?
  6. Case team leader: Certainly. Interviews can shape an evidence plan while remaining distinct from verified process findings.
  7. Analyst: I'll avoid writing no approval delays on the slide because that overstates the source.
  8. Case team leader: Use a precise label: one interview reported no approval delays; records have not yet been examined.
  9. Analyst: That preserves the useful observation and the uncertainty. I'll plan a targeted check rather than drop the question.
  10. Case team leader: Good. We'll update the hypothesis when the evidence warrants it, not when the first interview ends.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A frequent complaint is not yet the cause of churn

  2. Correct the ten gaps from the key. Reread the exchanges on overlapping mentions and evidence against the price hypothesis.

Interpret another overlapping interview sample

Forty customers departed. Ten volunteered for interviews; six mentioned onboarding and five mentioned support. Multiple answers were allowed. The sample has not been shown to represent every departing customer.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The interview sample contains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. ten customers

Ten departing customers were interviewed, not the full population of forty departures.

2. Manager: "Onboarding was mentioned by ___ of interviewees."

Answer and explanation

C. 60%

Six divided by ten equals sixty percent within this interview sample.

3. Analyst: "The categories contain ___."

Answer and explanation

D. overlapping mentions

Multiple answers were allowed, so onboarding and support may describe the same people.

4. Manager: "The result does not establish a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. causal driver

Topic frequency alone neither isolates the cause nor represents all departing customers.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Hypotheses and Issue Trees
Original fictional case: A client lost 60 accounts last quarter. Twenty departing customers volunteered for interviews: 12 mentioned price, 9 mentioned service delays, and 5 mentioned competitor features. Each person could mention more than one issue. Consultant Ada wants to lead with price as the cause of churn; manager Karim asks for a testable hypothesis and a structured inquiry into the alternatives. The interview sample is not established as representative. Timing, customer segments, actual prices paid, and comparable nondeparting accounts have not yet been analyzed. No causal driver has been isolated.
Language workshop: A frequent complaint is not yet the cause of churn
Communication goal: Use a working hypothesis to organize analysis while checking alternative explanations, overlapping categories, and evidence that could contradict it.
Language guidance: Twelve of twenty interviewees mentioned price: 60% of that interview group. It is not evidence that price caused 60% of all sixty departures. The price, service, and competitor counts overlap because respondents could give more than one answer.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Price is a working hypothesis, not an established churn driver. / We interviewed twenty of the sixty departing accounts. / Twelve of those twenty interviewees mentioned price. / Respondents could name more than one issue. / The categories are not mutually exclusive. / How would the proposed price effect lead to departure? / We also need to examine service experience and competitor changes. / What evidence would weaken the price hypothesis? / Did the price change precede the departure? / How do otherwise comparable nondeparting accounts differ? / Separate the patterns by relevant customer group. / Use prices actually paid, not only the published list price. / A frequent complaint does not by itself isolate a cause. / Start with questions that could change the client decision. / The new evidence may require us to change the initial explanation. / Assign a test, evidence source, and owner to each priority question.
Vocabulary: hypothesis: A proposed explanation that can be examined against evidence. / issue tree: A structured breakdown of a question into related subquestions. / hypothesis tree: A breakdown of a proposed explanation into testable supporting claims. / workstream: A coordinated part of the engagement's work. / churn: Customer or account loss, defined over a specified period and population. / churn driver: A factor contributing to customer departure. / exit interview: A conversation with someone leaving a service or organization. / multi-response question: A question allowing more than one answer category. / mention count: The number of recorded references to a specified topic. / sample: The observations included in an analysis. / sampling bias: Distortion associated with how observations enter a sample. / MECE: Mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive; a structuring aim of avoiding overlap and missing categories. / overlap: Inclusion of the same observation in more than one category. / double counting: Counting the same item more than intended in a total. / alternative explanation: A different possible account of an observed result. / disconfirming evidence: Information that weakens or contradicts a hypothesis. / confirmation bias: Favoring information that supports an existing belief. / causal mechanism: The process through which one factor could produce an effect. / segmentation: Dividing a population into defined groups for analysis. / cohort: A group sharing a defined starting characteristic or period. / price sensitivity: Responsiveness to price changes under specified conditions. / confounder: Another factor that complicates a proposed causal interpretation. / triangulation: Comparing different evidence sources or methods. / analytical priority: A question selected for attention based on decision relevance and evidence needs.
Speaking task: Read Ada and Karim aloud, then swap roles. Say the sample size before the sixty-percent figure. Correct the ten gaps from the key. Reread the exchanges on overlapping mentions and evidence against the price hypothesis. Repeat the client-update close using the supplied interview pattern and unresolved tests; keep a frequent complaint distinct from an established cause.
Partner: Ada: Consultant / Karim: Engagement manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Forty customers departed. Ten volunteered for interviews; six mentioned onboarding and five mentioned support. Multiple answers were allowed. The sample has not been shown to represent every departing customer.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ada: We can compare interview accounts, service records, and actual commercial terms to see where the evidence agrees or conflicts.
Karim: That is useful triangulation. Keep discrepancies visible. Agreement across sources may strengthen an interpretation, but it does not automatically remove all alternative explanations.
Ada: I will revise the update to state the interview pattern and the tests needed before recommending a price response.
Karim: Good. Set each analytical priority with an evidence source and owner. The initial hypothesis should help us learn faster, not make the team resistant to what the next analysis finds.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
