03Data Requests and Client BurdenForty requests, but only six are needed first+

Your goal

Prioritize a client data request, define the required measures, and agree a feasible delivery sequence without collecting unnecessary personal information.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Forty requests, but only six are needed first

Consultant Mateo sends client lead Leah a 40-item request due Friday. The first analysis actually needs six monthly measures for January through June at the North and South centers: completed cases, paid staff hours, labor cost, vendor cost, reopened cases, and opening backlog. That analysis is needed for a Wednesday workshop. Existing aggregate reports may be sufficient, but definitions and period coverage must be checked. The remaining 34 items have not been prioritized. Leah cannot promise the full request. Mateo must agree owners, feasible dates, and approved transfer arrangements rather than demand raw personal records by default.

Mateo: Consultant / Leah: Client data lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many items are required for the first analysis?

Answer and explanation

C. Six

The brief identifies six measures needed first and leaves the remaining thirty-four unprioritized.

2. Which period is required?

Answer and explanation

D. January through June, monthly, for both centers

The required structure is monthly coverage from January through June for North and South.

3. What is the timing problem?

Answer and explanation

D. A Friday deadline is later than the Wednesday workshop that needs the analysis.

The first analysis must support Wednesday, so its necessary inputs need a feasible earlier arrangement.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A smaller first request is not a waiver of definitions or quality. Confirm what completed, reopened, and opening mean in the existing reports, along with units, cost scope, and monthly coverage. A timely file with misunderstood fields can produce a misleading comparison.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
data request list
A documented set of information items requested for an analysis.prioritize the data request list
DRL
Data request list, with items, owners, definitions, and delivery status.update the DRL
minimum viable dataset
The smallest adequate set of information for a defined initial analysis.define the minimum viable dataset
data owner
The person or function responsible for the relevant information.identify the data owner
extract
A selected set of information taken from a source system.prepare a data extract
aggregate
Information combined into defined groups rather than individual records.use monthly aggregates
granularity
The level of detail represented in the data.agree the required granularity
reporting period
The time interval covered by a measure.specify the reporting period
data dictionary
Definitions of fields, units, and relevant rules.request a data dictionary
field definition
The meaning and permitted interpretation of a data item.confirm field definitions
unit of measure
The scale or quantity in which a value is expressed.preserve the unit of measure
coverage
The population, locations, or periods included.check period coverage
completeness
Whether the required information is present.assess completeness
reconciliation
A comparison used to explain or resolve differences between records.reconcile source totals
source system
The originating application or record for data.identify the source system
secure transfer
An approved method for moving information with appropriate protection.arrange secure transfer
access permission
Authorization to view or use specified information.confirm access permissions
personal data
Information relating to an identifiable individual under the applicable context.limit personal data
data minimization
Restricting collection to information necessary for the stated purpose.apply data minimization
request dependency
A condition that must be met before a requested item can be delivered or used.identify request dependencies
delivery sequence
The order in which information will be provided.agree the delivery sequence
staged request
A request divided into prioritized batches.use a staged request
exception log
A record of missing, changed, or unresolved items.maintain an exception log
version identifier
A label distinguishing one version of a file or dataset.record the version identifier

Aggregate reporting may answer the first question without individual records. Verify suitability and approved access rather than requesting personal data for possible future use. Where an item cannot arrive in time, state the effect on the analysis instead of quietly substituting a different measure.

C Notice the language

Client effort is part of the work plan

A data request competes with the daily responsibilities of the client team. Explain which decision each priority item supports, accept existing formats when suitable, and negotiate dates with the people doing the work. Labeling every item urgent usually makes genuine priorities harder to recognize.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the burden
The original list did not distinguish what we need first.
Explain the priority
Six measures support the first workshop analysis.
Specify the period
We need monthly figures from January through June for both centers.
Use existing work
Can the existing reports supply those measures?
Check the detail level
Monthly aggregates may be sufficient for this question.
Define the measure
What does completed case mean in this report?
Check cost scope
Do the labor and vendor figures cover the same operating boundary?
Assign an owner
Who can confirm the definition and supply each item?
Negotiate a date
What can realistically be available before Wednesday's workshop?
Stage the remainder
We will prioritize the other thirty-four items separately.
Avoid unnecessary collection
We do not need individual personal records for this first analysis.
Agree the transfer
Use the client-approved channel and access permissions.
Keep gaps visible
Please flag missing periods rather than filling them silently.
Track the version
Record the file version and extraction date.
State an analytical limit
If that measure is unavailable, we will explain the limitation in the workshop.
Close the request
I will circulate the agreed six-item list, owners, dates, and definitions.
6 language and precision notes
Did not distinguish
Acknowledges a weakness in the request without blaming the client.
Support the analysis
Explains why an item is being requested.
May be sufficient
Leaves suitability open to a definition and coverage check.
Realistically
Invites a feasible commitment rather than a pressured guess.
Separately
Stops unprioritized work inheriting the first deadline.
Rather than silently
Makes an omission visible before it becomes an analytical error.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which revised request is most useful?

Answer and explanation

C. Six defined monthly measures first, with agreed owners and dates; prioritize the rest separately.

A staged request aligns the immediate analysis with defined information, accountable owners, and feasible delivery.

2. What should happen if an existing report is available?

Answer and explanation

C. Check its definitions, scope, and coverage before accepting it.

Existing reports may reduce effort, but their fields and boundaries must fit the required analysis.

3. What if a priority item cannot arrive before the workshop?

Answer and explanation

C. Agree a realistic plan and state the resulting analytical limit.

A missing input changes what the analysis can support and should be disclosed rather than concealed.

4. Which information-handling choice fits the first task?

Answer and explanation

B. Use suitable aggregates through approved access and transfer arrangements.

The case calls for the necessary level of detail and authorized handling, not unrestricted collection or access.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Forty requests, but only six are needed first

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mateo: Consultant / Leah: Client data lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • data owner
  • reconciliation
  • granularity
  • data request list
  • data dictionary
  • minimum viable dataset
  • version identifier
  • secure transfer
  • delivery sequence
  • exception log
  1. Leah
    Forty items by Friday is more than my team can commit to during reporting close. Which files actually drive the first piece of analysis?
  2. Mateo
    You are right. The 1 did not distinguish what we need first. Only six measures support the initial analysis, and I should have separated them from the rest.
  3. Leah
    The workshop is Wednesday. A single Friday deadline does not help us understand what you need in time for that meeting.
  4. Mateo
    Let us agree a 2 with feasible dates. We need to know what can arrive before Wednesday and what limitations we will have if something is not available.
  5. Leah
    Please name the six measures. We may already have monthly reports that save the teams from producing new files.
  6. Mateo
    Our 3 is completed cases, paid staff hours, labor cost, vendor cost, reopened cases, and opening backlog for January through June at both centers.
  7. Leah
    We have monthly summaries, but the two sites may define completed and reopened differently. The report labels do not explain that.
  8. Mateo
    We need a 4 or equivalent definitions. Confirm what each field means and which period it covers before we compare figures that only appear to describe the same thing.
  9. Leah
    Would monthly totals by center be enough? Individual case records would require a different review and take longer to prepare.
  10. Mateo
    That 5 may be sufficient for the first question. Let us verify the report coverage and definitions before asking for more detailed data that we do not currently need.
  11. Leah
    There is a catch in labor cost: North includes a shared-service allocation. I need to check South before you compare the two totals.
  12. Mateo
    Please flag that for 6. We need the cost scope alongside the numbers. A difference in allocation rules could otherwise look like a difference in operating performance.
  13. Leah
    I can identify the relevant owners today. They need to confirm what can be supplied before the workshop, rather than have a date assigned without discussion.
  14. Mateo
    Agreed. Each 7 should confirm the definition and feasible delivery date. We will prioritize the other thirty-four items separately once their analytical purpose is clear.
  15. Leah
    Our team also needs to know how the files will be shared and which consultants are authorized to see them.
  16. Mateo
    We will use the approved 8 arrangements and access permissions. We do not need names or contact details for these monthly measures, so they should not be collected for this first analysis.
  17. Leah
    If a month is missing, should the team leave a note in the file rather than estimate it?
  18. Mateo
    Yes, keep an 9. Identify the gap and its owner. We will explain its effect on the workshop analysis instead of quietly replacing it with a different period or measure.
  19. Leah
    Once the owners confirm the first batch, I will send the agreed files with their extraction dates.
  20. Mateo
    Include the 10 as well. I will circulate the six-item request with definitions, owners, dates, and remaining gaps so both teams know exactly what has been agreed.

Answers and explanations
  1. data request list Turn 2. The list needs to distinguish the six immediate inputs from later, unprioritized work.
  2. delivery sequence Turn 4. The sequence puts workshop inputs first, with dates negotiated against actual client capacity.
  3. minimum viable dataset Turn 6. The six specified measures supply the initial analysis, rather than every possible future question.
  4. data dictionary Turn 8. Definitions prevent different site meanings from being treated as equivalent simply because labels match.
  5. granularity Turn 10. Suitable monthly center totals may answer the initial question without requiring individual case records.
  6. reconciliation Turn 12. The differing cost allocations need examination so unlike operating boundaries are not silently compared.
  7. data owner Turn 14. The owner confirms and supplies the information, supporting a realistic commitment and accurate definitions.
  8. secure transfer Turn 16. Files need the approved route and permissions, not any convenient communication channel.
  9. exception log Turn 18. The log records missing periods and unresolved definitions rather than silently replacing them.
  10. version identifier Turn 20. The identifier distinguishes supplied files from revisions, making the analysis and corrections traceable.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leah: Forty items by Friday is more than my team can commit to during reporting close. Which files actually drive the first piece of analysis?
  2. Mateo: You are right. The data request list did not distinguish what we need first. Only six measures support the initial analysis, and I should have separated them from the rest.
  3. Leah: The workshop is Wednesday. A single Friday deadline does not help us understand what you need in time for that meeting.
  4. Mateo: Let us agree a delivery sequence with feasible dates. We need to know what can arrive before Wednesday and what limitations we will have if something is not available.
  5. Leah: Please name the six measures. We may already have monthly reports that save the teams from producing new files.
  6. Mateo: Our minimum viable dataset is completed cases, paid staff hours, labor cost, vendor cost, reopened cases, and opening backlog for January through June at both centers.
  7. Leah: We have monthly summaries, but the two sites may define completed and reopened differently. The report labels do not explain that.
  8. Mateo: We need a data dictionary or equivalent definitions. Confirm what each field means and which period it covers before we compare figures that only appear to describe the same thing.
  9. Leah: Would monthly totals by center be enough? Individual case records would require a different review and take longer to prepare.
  10. Mateo: That granularity may be sufficient for the first question. Let us verify the report coverage and definitions before asking for more detailed data that we do not currently need.
  11. Leah: There is a catch in labor cost: North includes a shared-service allocation. I need to check South before you compare the two totals.
  12. Mateo: Please flag that for reconciliation. We need the cost scope alongside the numbers. A difference in allocation rules could otherwise look like a difference in operating performance.
  13. Leah: I can identify the relevant owners today. They need to confirm what can be supplied before the workshop, rather than have a date assigned without discussion.
  14. Mateo: Agreed. Each data owner should confirm the definition and feasible delivery date. We will prioritize the other thirty-four items separately once their analytical purpose is clear.
  15. Leah: Our team also needs to know how the files will be shared and which consultants are authorized to see them.
  16. Mateo: We will use the approved secure transfer arrangements and access permissions. We do not need names or contact details for these monthly measures, so they should not be collected for this first analysis.
  17. Leah: If a month is missing, should the team leave a note in the file rather than estimate it?
  18. Mateo: Yes, keep an exception log. Identify the gap and its owner. We will explain its effect on the workshop analysis instead of quietly replacing it with a different period or measure.
  19. Leah: Once the owners confirm the first batch, I will send the agreed files with their extraction dates.
  20. Mateo: Include the version identifier as well. I will circulate the six-item request with definitions, owners, dates, and remaining gaps so both teams know exactly what has been agreed.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Six items first, not forty at once

Separate fictional extension of the lesson: a forty-item request is due Friday. The first analysis needs January-March order dates, completion dates, service types, status codes, team identifiers, and a field dictionary to compare turnaround by service. Other items can be deferred by agreement.

  1. Engagement manager: Could you provide those six January-March items first, so we can compare turnaround by service before receiving everything?
  2. Client data coordinator: Our owner can prepare the dates, types, statuses, and team identifiers by Friday, but the dictionary needs Monday.
  3. Engagement manager: Could we receive the five available fields Friday and the dictionary Monday, with all remaining items deferred pending a separate schedule?
  4. Client data coordinator: Yes, but you must not interpret unfamiliar codes before the dictionary arrives. Some labels are ambiguous.
  5. Engagement manager: Agreed. We can check file structure Friday and wait until Monday to interpret the coded fields.
  6. Client data coordinator: Please use our approved secure transfer folder. No customer names are needed for this comparison.
  7. Engagement manager: Understood. Are you coordinating delivery with the data owner and flagging any availability change?
  8. Client data coordinator: Yes. I will confirm the staged request with the owner today.
  9. Engagement manager: Then five fields Friday, dictionary Monday, and a separately agreed schedule for the remaining thirty-four items.
  10. Client data coordinator: Correct. That gives us a manageable request with clear priorities and confidentiality boundaries.
A sample extract before a large pull

Separate fictional case: a consultant needs two years of tickets for a resolution-time study. First, ten synthetic examples representing January 2026 are requested with ticket ID, opened time, resolved time, status code, and a code dictionary. This sample will check structure and meaning, not measure performance.

  1. Consulting analyst: Could you provide ten synthetic January examples with the specified fields and code dictionary to validate structure before the two-year extract?
  2. Client reporting lead: Yes. Do you need customer names or message text as well?
  3. Consulting analyst: No. Ticket ID, opened time, resolved time, status code, and the dictionary are sufficient for this initial check.
  4. Client reporting lead: Our export owner can prepare the examples by Thursday. Would that fit your review?
  5. Consulting analyst: Thursday works. Please identify the timestamp time zone and show how unresolved tickets appear, rather than filling missing resolution times.
  6. Client reporting lead: Understood. The synthetic sample will illustrate those cases without exposing customer information.
  7. Consulting analyst: Could you confirm the owner and delivery route so we can arrange the review?
  8. Client reporting lead: I will coordinate delivery through the approved project folder and flag any delay.
  9. Consulting analyst: Thank you. We will validate field meanings, not calculate real resolution performance from synthetic examples.
  10. Client reporting lead: Agreed. After that check, we can discuss availability and timing for the larger authorized extract.
An unavailable field should not become a guess

Separate fictional case: a client can provide monthly totals but cannot currently export transaction-level timestamps requested for a cycle-time analysis.

  1. Workstream consultant: You can provide monthly totals, but the requested transaction timestamps are unavailable. Could you confirm the export limitation?
  2. Client systems manager: Yes. We have the totals, but we haven't established a way to extract the detailed timestamps.
  3. Workstream consultant: Thank you. Please don't estimate transaction times to fill the requested field; that would change the analysis basis.
  4. Client systems manager: Could the team use monthly totals instead, so we can meet the original deadline?
  5. Workstream consultant: They may support a different question, but they don't substitute for individual cycle-time measurements.
  6. Client systems manager: Then we need to discuss whether to change the analysis or investigate another authorized source.
  7. Workstream consultant: Exactly. Could you identify who can assess another source and estimate the work involved?
  8. Client systems manager: I'll ask the system owner and return with availability, rather than promise a detailed extract now.
  9. Workstream consultant: I'll explain the impact to the engagement lead and keep the missing evidence visible.
  10. Client systems manager: That gives us a specific next request without creating data that the system hasn't supplied.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Forty requests, but only six are needed first
  1. Read Leah and Mateo aloud, then swap roles. Give the six priority measures as a clear list.
  2. Check all ten gaps. Reread the period, definition, aggregation, and approved-transfer exchanges using the corrected wording.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Stage another information request

A 25-item request contains four inputs needed for a Monday review. Existing weekly summaries may supply them. The remaining 21 items are for later analysis. Client owners must confirm definitions and realistic delivery dates.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Consultant: "The first batch contains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. four inputs

Four specified inputs support the immediate review, not all twenty-five requested items.

2. Client: "The first review is on ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Monday

Monday is the stated review date that the first delivery sequence must support.

3. Consultant: "The later group contains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. twenty-one items

Subtracting four priority inputs from twenty-five leaves twenty-one items for later prioritization.

4. Client: "Before using existing summaries, confirm their ___."

Answer and explanation

B. definitions

Available files must match the intended measures; their existence alone does not establish suitability.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 03: Data Requests and Client Burden
Original fictional case: Consultant Mateo sends client lead Leah a 40-item request due Friday. The first analysis actually needs six monthly measures for January through June at the North and South centers: completed cases, paid staff hours, labor cost, vendor cost, reopened cases, and opening backlog. That analysis is needed for a Wednesday workshop. Existing aggregate reports may be sufficient, but definitions and period coverage must be checked. The remaining 34 items have not been prioritized. Leah cannot promise the full request. Mateo must agree owners, feasible dates, and approved transfer arrangements rather than demand raw personal records by default.
Language workshop: Forty requests, but only six are needed first
Communication goal: Prioritize a client data request, define the required measures, and agree a feasible delivery sequence without collecting unnecessary personal information.
Language guidance: A smaller first request is not a waiver of definitions or quality. Confirm what completed, reopened, and opening mean in the existing reports, along with units, cost scope, and monthly coverage. A timely file with misunderstood fields can produce a misleading comparison.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The original list did not distinguish what we need first. / Six measures support the first workshop analysis. / We need monthly figures from January through June for both centers. / Can the existing reports supply those measures? / Monthly aggregates may be sufficient for this question. / What does completed case mean in this report? / Do the labor and vendor figures cover the same operating boundary? / Who can confirm the definition and supply each item? / What can realistically be available before Wednesday's workshop? / We will prioritize the other thirty-four items separately. / We do not need individual personal records for this first analysis. / Use the client-approved channel and access permissions. / Please flag missing periods rather than filling them silently. / Record the file version and extraction date. / If that measure is unavailable, we will explain the limitation in the workshop. / I will circulate the agreed six-item list, owners, dates, and definitions.
Vocabulary: data request list: A documented set of information items requested for an analysis. / DRL: Data request list, with items, owners, definitions, and delivery status. / minimum viable dataset: The smallest adequate set of information for a defined initial analysis. / data owner: The person or function responsible for the relevant information. / extract: A selected set of information taken from a source system. / aggregate: Information combined into defined groups rather than individual records. / granularity: The level of detail represented in the data. / reporting period: The time interval covered by a measure. / data dictionary: Definitions of fields, units, and relevant rules. / field definition: The meaning and permitted interpretation of a data item. / unit of measure: The scale or quantity in which a value is expressed. / coverage: The population, locations, or periods included. / completeness: Whether the required information is present. / reconciliation: A comparison used to explain or resolve differences between records. / source system: The originating application or record for data. / secure transfer: An approved method for moving information with appropriate protection. / access permission: Authorization to view or use specified information. / personal data: Information relating to an identifiable individual under the applicable context. / data minimization: Restricting collection to information necessary for the stated purpose. / request dependency: A condition that must be met before a requested item can be delivered or used. / delivery sequence: The order in which information will be provided. / staged request: A request divided into prioritized batches. / exception log: A record of missing, changed, or unresolved items. / version identifier: A label distinguishing one version of a file or dataset.
Speaking task: Read Leah and Mateo aloud, then swap roles. Give the six priority measures as a clear list. Check all ten gaps. Reread the period, definition, aggregation, and approved-transfer exchanges using the corrected wording. Repeat the agreed first-batch close with named owners and feasible dates; keep the remaining thirty-four items outside the confirmed first delivery.
Partner: Mateo: Consultant / Leah: Client data lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A 25-item request contains four inputs needed for a Monday review. Existing weekly summaries may supply them. The remaining 21 items are for later analysis. Client owners must confirm definitions and realistic delivery dates.

Mateo: Include the version identifier as well. I will circulate the six-item request with definitions, owners, dates, and remaining gaps so both teams know exactly what has been agreed.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Slide Storylines and Executive SynthesisTwelve charts need one clear decision request+

Your goal

Convert an analytical presentation into a supported recommendation with a specific request, visible limits, and an agreed test.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Twelve charts need one clear decision request

Analyst Jun has twelve charts about a proposed case-routing process but no clear recommendation. Engagement manager Sofia must prepare an executive discussion about a pilot. Walkthroughs suggest a possible benefit, but there is no live operating result. The proposal is a four-week pilot at one center with a $20,000 budget request. Current median handling time is 12 minutes. Proposed success measures are a median of 10 minutes or less and an error rate no higher than 2%, using agreed definitions and eligible-case coverage. The committee is being asked to authorize the pilot, not full rollout.

Jun: Analyst / Sofia: Engagement manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What authorization is requested?

Answer and explanation

A. A four-week, one-center pilot with a $20,000 budget

The proposal specifies a bounded pilot and budget, not organization-wide implementation.

2. What evidence exists so far?

Answer and explanation

D. Walkthroughs suggesting a possible benefit, with no live operating result

The brief identifies walkthrough evidence and explicitly states that live operating results are absent.

3. What are the proposed success measures?

Answer and explanation

C. Median handling time at most 10 minutes and error rate at most 2%

The proposed test combines the stated median-time threshold with an error-rate guardrail under agreed definitions.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twelve minutes is the current median, not necessarily the mean. Ten minutes is a proposed threshold, not an achieved result. The 2% error guardrail needs a defined numerator, denominator, and eligible-case population so faster work does not conceal a quality problem.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
storyline
The logical sequence connecting a presentation's messages.build the storyline
executive synthesis
A concise integration of findings and implications for a decision.prepare an executive synthesis
governing message
The central point organizing the supporting argument.state the governing message
action title
A slide heading expressing its key conclusion or implication.use an action title
exhibit
A chart, table, or other analytical display.reference the exhibit
finding
An observation established by the analysis.state the finding
insight
An interpretation explaining why a finding matters.develop an insight
implication
A consequence or significance for the decision.explain the implication
recommendation
A proposed course of action with its supporting basis.make a recommendation
decision ask
The specific authorization or choice requested from the audience.clarify the decision ask
supporting evidence
Information used to substantiate a statement.present supporting evidence
evidence chain
The connection from source information through findings to a conclusion.check the evidence chain
caveat
A material limitation or condition on a claim.retain the caveat
main body
The principal part of a presentation, excluding supporting appendices.keep limits in the main body
appendix
Supporting detail placed after the main material.move detail to the appendix
pre-read
Material distributed for review before a meeting.circulate the pre-read
visual hierarchy
The arrangement that signals the relative importance of displayed information.improve the visual hierarchy
chart annotation
A note explaining a feature or limit of a chart.add a chart annotation
denominator
The total underlying a proportion or rate.state the denominator
median
The middle value in ordered observations, or the midpoint of two middle values.report the median
pilot
A limited test of an approach before a wider decision.authorize a pilot
guardrail
A defined limit intended to prevent an unacceptable side effect.set a quality guardrail
success criterion
A specified condition used to judge the result.agree success criteria
rollout decision
A choice about extending an approach beyond the initial test.separate the rollout decision

A pilot recommendation can be clear even when the eventual benefit is uncertain. Ask for the limited test, explain why the evidence justifies testing, and identify what the test must show. Do not present authorization to learn as authorization for full rollout.

C Notice the language

Concise does not mean unqualified

A senior audience may interrupt a long tour of the analysis to ask what it should do. Lead with the requested decision, then give the evidence and material limits. Put supporting detail in the appendix, but keep information that changes the decision visible in the main presentation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the request
We recommend a four-week pilot at one center.
State the amount
The budget request is twenty thousand dollars.
Limit the authorization
We are not asking for full rollout today.
State the evidence level
Walkthroughs suggest a possible benefit; live results are not yet available.
Explain the implication
That supports a bounded test, not a claim of proven operating improvement.
Name the current measure
The current median handling time is twelve minutes.
Name the proposed threshold
The pilot target is a median of ten minutes or less.
Preserve quality
The error rate must be no higher than two percent under the agreed definition.
Define the measurement
Specify eligible cases, the error numerator, and the denominator before launch.
Improve the heading
Make the slide title state the finding, not just the chart topic.
Connect the argument
What does this exhibit change about the decision?
Keep the caveat visible
The lack of live evidence belongs in the main presentation.
Move supporting detail
Put the calculation detail in the appendix, with a clear reference.
Avoid selective emphasis
Show the quality measure alongside the speed result.
Separate later decisions
A wider rollout requires a subsequent review and authorization.
Close the presentation
Ask the committee to approve the pilot scope, budget, and measurement plan.
6 language and precision notes
Recommend
States a position without pretending the choice is already authorized.
Not asking for
Limits the immediate decision.
Suggest
Describes preliminary evidence without upgrading it to proof.
Must be no higher
Establishes a proposed upper limit rather than a descriptive average.
Alongside
Prevents one favorable measure from hiding another important result.
Subsequent
Makes the later rollout decision distinct from this pilot approval.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is decision-focused and accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Approve a four-week, one-center pilot for $20,000 to test the proposed process.

The accurate opening states the bounded authorization being sought without inventing achieved results.

2. Which statement belongs in the main presentation?

Answer and explanation

B. No live operating result is available yet.

The absence of live evidence materially affects the decision and should not be buried in supporting material.

3. Which comparison preserves the metric?

Answer and explanation

B. Current median 12 minutes; proposed pilot threshold at most 10 minutes

The comparison keeps the stated summary measure and distinguishes current performance from a proposed threshold.

4. What prevents speed from becoming the only success measure?

Answer and explanation

B. Report the defined error-rate guardrail alongside handling time.

The proposed pilot requires both speed and quality measures over the agreed eligible-case population.

Twelve charts need one clear decision request

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jun: Analyst / Sofia: Engagement manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Jun
    Twelve charts, and I still cannot tell what I want the committee to approve until the last slide. Can we rebuild the opening around the decision?
  2. Sofia
    Start with the 1. We want authorization for a four-week pilot at one center, with a twenty-thousand-dollar budget. The audience should not have to infer that from the exhibits.
  3. Jun
    Should the opening say the new routing process will reduce handling time, or is that stronger than our evidence supports?
  4. Sofia
    Keep the 2 bounded. Walkthroughs suggest a possible benefit, but there is no live operating result yet. We recommend testing the process, not claiming that the improvement has already happened.
  5. Jun
    Then the next page should explain why testing is reasonable and what uncertainty the pilot would address.
  6. Sofia
    That gives us a 3. Each exhibit should serve that argument. If a chart does not affect the decision or explain the test, it probably does not need equal time in the meeting.
  7. Jun
    The current handling-time chart says twelve minutes. I need to label that as the median, not just call it the average.
  8. Sofia
    Yes, preserve the 4. The proposed threshold is ten minutes or less on the same defined measure. Do not make a target sound like an achieved result or a promise about every individual case.
  9. Jun
    The error measure is on a separate page. The committee might hear the faster-time target and miss the quality condition.
  10. Sofia
    Bring the 5 alongside it. The proposed error rate must be no higher than two percent under the agreed definition. Faster work is not enough if quality deteriorates beyond that limit.
  11. Jun
    We should define eligible cases and how errors are counted before launching, rather than adjust the measures after seeing the result.
  12. Sofia
    Exactly. State the 6 and measurement plan up front. The numerator, denominator, and case coverage need to be clear so the committee knows what a favorable result would actually mean.
  13. Jun
    My title just says Routing Analysis. It tells them the topic, but not what the walkthrough found or why we still need a pilot.
  14. Sofia
    Use an 7 that communicates the implication accurately. For example, the evidence supports a limited live test. The heading must not imply proven improvement while the detail says otherwise.
  15. Jun
    I can move the detailed walkthrough calculations behind the main story, with a reference for anyone who wants to inspect them.
  16. Sofia
    The 8 is appropriate for that detail. But keep the lack of live results and the quality condition in the main presentation, because they change how the recommendation should be understood.
  17. Jun
    If the committee approves the pilot, the minutes should not say that it approved the entire new operating model.
  18. Sofia
    Correct. A 9 comes later, after the relevant results and review. We should state that distinction in both the request and the closing summary.
  19. Jun
    I will reorder the deck around the request, evidence, proposed test, and decision conditions, with the detailed calculations available separately.
  20. Sofia
    That is an 10. It is shorter because the argument is organized, not because important uncertainty has been removed.

Answers and explanations
  1. decision ask Turn 2. The audience needs the specific pilot scope and budget request before the supporting charts.
  2. recommendation Turn 4. The recommendation proposes a test; walkthroughs do not support a promise of live improvement.
  3. storyline Turn 6. The storyline connects the request, evidence, uncertainty, and test rather than merely arranging charts by topic.
  4. median Turn 8. The stated median must not be relabeled as the mean or a value applying to every case.
  5. guardrail Turn 10. The error limit stays visible alongside speed, preventing a one-sided definition of pilot success.
  6. success criteria Turn 12. The thresholds need agreed measurement rules and eligible-case coverage before results are observed.
  7. action title Turn 14. The heading states the supported implication without claiming more than the exhibit establishes.
  8. appendix Turn 16. Supporting calculations can move here; decision-changing evidence limits must remain in the main presentation.
  9. rollout decision Turn 18. Wider implementation needs a separate later authorization; pilot approval does not automatically permit it.
  10. executive synthesis Turn 20. Synthesis connects findings to a bounded decision instead of giving every analysis equal emphasis.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Twelve charts and no decision ask

The lesson's presentation contains twelve charts but no clear recommendation; executives must decide whether to pilot a new process.

  1. Presentation reviewer: We have twelve charts, but what decision are we asking the executives to make about the pilot?
  2. Workstream lead: Whether to pilot the new process. The deck describes findings without making that decision ask explicit.
  3. Presentation reviewer: Then the storyline needs to connect evidence to a recommendation, not simply display all the analysis.
  4. Workstream lead: Could you explain the difference between a recommendation and the decision ask in this context?
  5. Presentation reviewer: The recommendation proposes action; the decision ask identifies what the audience must authorize or resolve.
  6. Workstream lead: So a chart title describes a result, while its so what explains why that result matters for the pilot.
  7. Presentation reviewer: Exactly. We still need to qualify any conclusion that the charts don't fully support.
  8. Workstream lead: I'll organize the spoken readout around the pilot question and keep detailed charts available for follow-up.
  9. Presentation reviewer: Good. Don't invent an approval recommendation before the evidence and remaining constraints have been considered.
  10. Workstream lead: I'll make the choice and open questions clear, then verify that the proposed storyline matches the analysis.
A finding is not yet a recommendation

Separate fictional case: a process study finds duplicate entry in two systems, but implementation costs and system constraints have not been assessed.

  1. Senior consultant: The finding is duplicate entry across two systems. Your slide recommends immediate automation, but feasibility hasn't been assessed.
  2. Analyst: I thought the recommendation was simply the practical version of the finding. What distinction am I missing?
  3. Senior consultant: The finding describes observed work. A recommendation proposes action and needs support about consequences and constraints.
  4. Analyst: Then the so what can identify possible avoidable effort without claiming automation is already the right solution.
  5. Senior consultant: Correct. We haven't assessed implementation cost or system constraints, so the next decision may concern further evaluation.
  6. Analyst: Could the decision ask be to authorize a feasibility assessment, provided the engagement lead agrees that's appropriate?
  7. Senior consultant: Yes, as a proposed next step. Label it clearly rather than imply executives have already approved automation.
  8. Analyst: I'll separate observed duplication, its possible significance, and the remaining feasibility questions in the storyline.
  9. Senior consultant: That clarifies the presentation and avoids a jump from evidence to unsupported action.
  10. Analyst: I'll bring the revised logic for review before changing the executive recommendation.
A headline that hides the tradeoff

Separate fictional case: two service options are being compared; A has a shorter stated rollout and B a lower quoted setup fee, while recurring costs remain unreviewed.

  1. Engagement director: Your headline says option A is best, but the analysis only shows a shorter stated rollout.
  2. Consultant: Option B has a lower setup fee. I left that detail in a supporting chart.
  3. Engagement director: Then the storyline hides a material tradeoff. Best needs criteria, not just a preferred feature.
  4. Consultant: Would a clearer executive synthesis say A is faster to roll out, while B costs less to set up?
  5. Engagement director: Yes, with the stated basis. Recurring costs remain unreviewed, so don't call B cheaper overall.
  6. Consultant: The recommendation should explain which priority it serves and what the decision maker gives up.
  7. Engagement director: Exactly. The decision ask should then identify the choice or additional evidence needed.
  8. Consultant: I'll keep the unknown recurring costs next to the comparison rather than hide them in an appendix.
  9. Engagement director: Good. A concise story can still preserve uncertainty and the weaker side of a proposal.
  10. Consultant: I'll revise the spoken summary so executives hear the contrast before discussing a preferred option.

State another bounded pilot request

A team proposes a three-week trial at two branches with a $12,000 budget. No live results exist. The committee is asked to authorize the trial only; broader rollout requires another decision.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Presenter: "The proposed duration is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. three weeks

Three weeks defines the requested trial period, not a permanent implementation.

2. Chair: "The trial covers ___."

Answer and explanation

B. two branches

The specified two-branch boundary limits the scope of the requested authorization.

3. Presenter: "The budget request is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. $12,000

Twelve thousand dollars is the amount supplied for this limited trial.

4. Chair: "Broader rollout requires ___."

Answer and explanation

C. another decision

Trial approval does not automatically authorize expansion beyond the stated scope.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 04: Slide Storylines and Executive Synthesis
Original fictional case: Analyst Jun has twelve charts about a proposed case-routing process but no clear recommendation. Engagement manager Sofia must prepare an executive discussion about a pilot. Walkthroughs suggest a possible benefit, but there is no live operating result. The proposal is a four-week pilot at one center with a $20,000 budget request. Current median handling time is 12 minutes. Proposed success measures are a median of 10 minutes or less and an error rate no higher than 2%, using agreed definitions and eligible-case coverage. The committee is being asked to authorize the pilot, not full rollout.
Language workshop: Twelve charts need one clear decision request
Communication goal: Convert an analytical presentation into a supported recommendation with a specific request, visible limits, and an agreed test.
Language guidance: Twelve minutes is the current median, not necessarily the mean. Ten minutes is a proposed threshold, not an achieved result. The 2% error guardrail needs a defined numerator, denominator, and eligible-case population so faster work does not conceal a quality problem.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): We recommend a four-week pilot at one center. / The budget request is twenty thousand dollars. / We are not asking for full rollout today. / Walkthroughs suggest a possible benefit; live results are not yet available. / That supports a bounded test, not a claim of proven operating improvement. / The current median handling time is twelve minutes. / The pilot target is a median of ten minutes or less. / The error rate must be no higher than two percent under the agreed definition. / Specify eligible cases, the error numerator, and the denominator before launch. / Make the slide title state the finding, not just the chart topic. / What does this exhibit change about the decision? / The lack of live evidence belongs in the main presentation. / Put the calculation detail in the appendix, with a clear reference. / Show the quality measure alongside the speed result. / A wider rollout requires a subsequent review and authorization. / Ask the committee to approve the pilot scope, budget, and measurement plan.
Vocabulary: storyline: The logical sequence connecting a presentation's messages. / executive synthesis: A concise integration of findings and implications for a decision. / governing message: The central point organizing the supporting argument. / action title: A slide heading expressing its key conclusion or implication. / exhibit: A chart, table, or other analytical display. / finding: An observation established by the analysis. / insight: An interpretation explaining why a finding matters. / implication: A consequence or significance for the decision. / recommendation: A proposed course of action with its supporting basis. / decision ask: The specific authorization or choice requested from the audience. / supporting evidence: Information used to substantiate a statement. / evidence chain: The connection from source information through findings to a conclusion. / caveat: A material limitation or condition on a claim. / main body: The principal part of a presentation, excluding supporting appendices. / appendix: Supporting detail placed after the main material. / pre-read: Material distributed for review before a meeting. / visual hierarchy: The arrangement that signals the relative importance of displayed information. / chart annotation: A note explaining a feature or limit of a chart. / denominator: The total underlying a proportion or rate. / median: The middle value in ordered observations, or the midpoint of two middle values. / pilot: A limited test of an approach before a wider decision. / guardrail: A defined limit intended to prevent an unacceptable side effect. / success criterion: A specified condition used to judge the result. / rollout decision: A choice about extending an approach beyond the initial test.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Vocabulary: storyline: The logical sequence connecting a presentation's messages. / executive synthesis: A concise integration of findings and implications for a decision. / governing message: The central point organizing the supporting argument. / action title: A slide heading expressing its key conclusion or implication. / exhibit: A chart, table, or other analytical display. / finding: An observation established by the analysis. / insight: An interpretation explaining why a finding matters. / implication: A consequence or significance for the decision. / recommendation: A proposed course of action with its supporting basis. / decision ask: The specific authorization or choice requested from the audience. / supporting evidence: Information used to substantiate a statement. / evidence chain: The connection from source information through findings to a conclusion. / caveat: A material limitation or condition on a claim. / main body: The principal part of a presentation, excluding supporting appendices. / appendix: Supporting detail placed after the main material. / pre-read: Material distributed for review before a meeting. / visual hierarchy: The arrangement that signals the relative importance of displayed information. / chart annotation: A note explaining a feature or limit of a chart. / denominator: The total underlying a proportion or rate. / median: The middle value in ordered observations, or the midpoint of two middle values. / pilot: A limited test of an approach before a wider decision. / guardrail: A defined limit intended to prevent an unacceptable side effect. / success criterion: A specified condition used to judge the result. / rollout decision: A choice about extending an approach beyond the initial test.
Speaking task: Read Jun and Sofia aloud, then swap roles. Put the pilot request before the supporting detail. Check all ten gaps. Reread the median-time target and error ceiling together so neither sounds optional. Give the closing request again with the supplied duration, site, budget, and measurement conditions; do not turn pilot approval into rollout approval.
Partner: Jun: Analyst / Sofia: Engagement manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A team proposes a three-week trial at two branches with a $12,000 budget. No live results exist. The committee is asked to authorize the trial only; broader rollout requires another decision.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jun: If the committee approves the pilot, the minutes should not say that it approved the entire new operating model.
Sofia: Correct. A rollout decision comes later, after the relevant results and review. We should state that distinction in both the request and the closing summary.
Jun: I will reorder the deck around the request, evidence, proposed test, and decision conditions, with the detailed calculations available separately.
Sofia: That is an executive synthesis. It is shorter because the argument is organized, not because important uncertainty has been removed.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

05Scope Management and Change RequestsA second market is a new request, not a free extension+

Your goal

Respond constructively to additional work by distinguishing the agreed scope from the proposed change and explaining options without premature commitments.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A second market is a new request, not a free extension

Engagement C18 covers a six-week study of Market A for a fixed $60,000 fee. At the end of week three, client sponsor Helen asks for the same full study of Market B by the original deadline. The current team cannot complete both full studies in the remaining time. Consultant Raj can prepare options: additional resources subject to availability, an extension, a narrower Market B scan, or deferral. Fees and dates for the change are not yet assessed. The signed terms require an agreed written change before extra work begins. Helen has not approved a change.

Helen: Client sponsor / Raj: Engagement manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the signed engagement currently cover?

Answer and explanation

B. A six-week study of Market A for $60,000

Market A is the agreed scope; Market B is an additional request not covered by an approved change.

2. Can the current team finish both full studies by the original deadline?

Answer and explanation

C. No, not under the stated capacity.

The brief explicitly states that the current team cannot complete both full studies in the remaining time.

3. What must precede extra work?

Answer and explanation

B. An agreed written change under the signed terms

The fictional contract requires the written change to be agreed before extra work starts.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A fixed fee applies to the defined scope, not automatically to any additional request. The $60,000 figure is the existing Market A fee. No price or delivery date has yet been established for Market B, and the exercise supplies no basis for inventing one.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
scope baseline
The agreed work boundaries against which changes are assessed.protect the scope baseline
statement of work
A document specifying the work, outputs, and relevant engagement terms.review the statement of work
fixed fee
A specified charge for a defined scope under the agreement.confirm the fixed fee
change request
A proposal to alter agreed work or arrangements.raise a change request
scope creep
Uncontrolled growth in work beyond the agreed scope.prevent scope creep
impact assessment
Examination of a proposed change's consequences.complete an impact assessment
resource capacity
The work a team can perform with its available people and time.assess resource capacity
resource availability
Whether suitable people or other resources can be assigned when needed.check resource availability
deliverable
A specified output to be supplied.define the deliverable
acceptance criterion
A condition used to determine whether an output meets the agreement.agree acceptance criteria
change authorization
Formal permission for the specified change.obtain change authorization
commercial terms
Agreed conditions concerning fees and other business arrangements.confirm commercial terms
schedule extension
An approved increase in the time allowed.propose a schedule extension
scope trade-off
Exchanging one work element for another under agreed conditions.explain a scope trade-off
high-level scan
A limited overview rather than a full detailed study.offer a high-level scan
depth of analysis
The detail and rigor of examination provided.specify the depth of analysis
assumption log
A record of conditions used in planning or analysis.update the assumption log
dependency
A condition or task on which another depends.identify a dependency
rework
Work needed to revise or repeat previous output.estimate rework
change register
A record of requested changes and their status.maintain the change register
pending approval
Awaiting the relevant authorized decision.mark the change pending approval
deferral
Moving a decision or activity to a later time.propose deferral
out-of-scope request
A request beyond the currently agreed work.assess an out-of-scope request
written variation
A documented agreed change to specified engagement terms.execute a written variation

A high-level scan is not the same deliverable as a full market study. Specify coverage, evidence depth, exclusions, and acceptance conditions if that option is developed. Calling a narrower product a full study would conceal the trade-off rather than solve it.

C Notice the language

A boundary is easier to accept with choices

A blunt refusal can miss an important client need, while an unqualified yes can create an impossible commitment. Acknowledge the purpose of the request, explain the current boundary and capacity, and offer options for an authorized decision. Keep assessment separate from approval.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the purpose
I understand why Market B has become important.
State the current agreement
The signed scope covers the six-week Market A study.
Name the additional request
A full Market B study is a change to that scope.
Describe capacity honestly
The current team cannot complete both full studies by the existing deadline.
Offer assessment
We can assess options for resources, timing, and analytical depth.
Avoid an early quote
The revised fee and dates have not yet been assessed.
Check people before promising
Additional resources depend on suitable people being available.
Explain an extension
More time is one option, subject to agreement.
Distinguish a smaller product
A high-level scan would have narrower coverage than a full study.
Offer deferral
We could schedule Market B as a later phase.
Preserve existing commitments
We should show what each option does to Market A.
State the approval condition
The terms require an agreed written change before extra work starts.
Keep status accurate
The request is being assessed, not yet approved.
Define acceptance
Agree what the revised deliverable must contain.
Record the decision
Capture the chosen scope, fee, dates, and responsibilities in the variation.
Close without overpromising
I will return with assessed choices rather than commit to an unsupported deadline.
6 language and precision notes
Has become important
Acknowledges the new need without accepting the work automatically.
Current team
Makes the capacity statement specific rather than claiming no solution exists.
Have not yet
Identifies an assessment gap, not a refusal to discuss options.
Narrower coverage
States the real difference in the smaller deliverable.
Before extra work
Preserves the agreed approval sequence.
Assessed choices
Promises an evaluation process rather than an unknown result.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response is constructive and accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. We can assess ways to add Market B, but both full studies do not fit the current capacity and deadline.

The response acknowledges the need while making the known capacity limit and assessment step explicit.

2. Which statement about the scan is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

D. Its reduced coverage and depth must be specified.

A smaller product is a meaningful option only when its boundaries and expected output are clear.

3. What is the status of Market B?

Answer and explanation

B. Requested and awaiting assessment and written agreement

No assessment or agreed written variation exists, so the request remains unapproved additional work.

4. What should an impact assessment include?

Answer and explanation

C. Effects on scope, people, fees, timing, and existing commitments

A decision requires the practical and commercial consequences, including what happens to the original engagement.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

Answers and explanations
  1. scope baseline Turn 2. The baseline covers Market A; adding Market B changes the agreed work boundaries.
  2. resource capacity Turn 4. The current team cannot complete both full studies by the original deadline, despite possible method reuse.
  3. impact assessment Turn 6. The assessment compares effects on people, time, fees, scope, and the original commitments.
  4. resource availability Turn 8. Suitable extra staff must be verified before they can support a promised deadline.
  5. high-level scan Turn 10. The scan is a narrower product with different coverage and evidence depth from a full study.
  6. scope trade-off Turn 12. Any reduction or exchange in the original work must be explicit, not silently absorbed.
  7. change authorization Turn 14. The agreement requires a written approved change before the additional work starts.
  8. commercial terms Turn 16. The new fee remains unassessed; the existing $60,000 is not a Market B quotation.
  9. acceptance criteria Turn 18. These define what the revised output must contain so its narrower scope can be reviewed fairly.
  10. change register Turn 20. The register tracks the request's status, distinguishing assessed options from an agreed written variation.
Read the complete conversation

  9. Helen: We might accept less depth for Market B if it gives us an initial view before the committee meeting.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A second market changes the engagement

The lesson's client requests a second market study midway through an engagement scoped and scheduled for one market.

  1. Client sponsor: Could you add a second market study? We'd like it included in the current engagement readout.
  2. Engagement manager: We can assess that change request, but the current schedule and statement of work cover only the original market.
  3. Client sponsor: Could the team work faster so the additional scope doesn't affect either timing or cost?
  4. Engagement manager: I can't assume that. We need to assess the extra work and discuss an explicit tradeoff.
  5. Client sponsor: What options could we consider without committing to an unreviewed extension right now?
  6. Engagement manager: We could assess a later second study or a revised first-phase scope, subject to agreement.
  7. Client sponsor: Please show what each option includes and what would be deferred rather than just presenting a new fee.
  8. Engagement manager: Agreed. Any schedule or scope change needs the appropriate approval before the team treats it as committed.
  9. Client sponsor: I'll keep the request as a proposal while you estimate the implications for our decision needs.
  10. Engagement manager: I'll return with bounded alternatives and maintain the original plan until a change is agreed.
An extra workshop without extra preparation time

Separate fictional case: a consulting engagement includes one leadership workshop; the client asks for a separate frontline workshop before the same readout, with no additional capacity confirmed.

  1. Client project lead: Could you add a frontline workshop before the readout? The scope currently includes only the leadership session.
  2. Consulting manager: We can discuss it. A different audience may require different preparation, so it isn't simply another calendar invitation.
  3. Client project lead: Would using the same materials let you deliver both without changing the agreed schedule?
  4. Consulting manager: Possibly, but we'd need to assess whether those materials answer the frontline group's needs appropriately.
  5. Client project lead: Could we reduce the leadership session to make room, rather than add unplanned work?
  6. Consulting manager: That's an option to review, provided the sponsor accepts what the shorter session would no longer cover.
  7. Client project lead: Please compare a scope swap with a later additional workshop and make the conditions visible.
  8. Consulting manager: I will. Neither option is confirmed until we check preparation effort and obtain the relevant agreement.
  9. Client project lead: I'll avoid promising the frontline team a date until that change request is settled.
  10. Consulting manager: Thank you. We'll propose a useful tradeoff instead of silently expanding the statement of work.
Translation for a new audience

Separate fictional case: a final report is scoped in English; the client requests a second-language presentation, but translation review and presenter availability are unassessed.

  1. Client communications lead: Could the final presentation also be delivered in another language? Our current scope specifies English only.
  2. Engagement director: We can assess the request, but translation review and suitable presenter availability haven't been established.
  3. Client communications lead: Could you translate the slides automatically and keep the original delivery date without further review?
  4. Engagement director: I wouldn't promise that. The translated meaning and delivery arrangement need checking, especially for qualified findings.
  5. Client communications lead: Then what bounded alternative could help us discuss the results with that audience sooner?
  6. Engagement director: We could assess a translated summary or a later interpreted discussion, with scope and review requirements explicit.
  7. Client communications lead: Please show the tradeoff between the amount of material and the preparation time each option needs.
  8. Engagement director: Agreed. We'll keep the existing deliverable unchanged unless the authorized change request is approved.
  9. Client communications lead: I'll clarify the audience's actual need before we select one of those alternatives.
  10. Engagement director: That will help us propose a proportionate change without promising unverified timing or language quality.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.
