Lesson 05: Scope Management and Change Requests
Original fictional case: Engagement C18 covers a six-week study of Market A for a fixed $60,000 fee. At the end of week three, client sponsor Helen asks for the same full study of Market B by the original deadline. The current team cannot complete both full studies in the remaining time. Consultant Raj can prepare options: additional resources subject to availability, an extension, a narrower Market B scan, or deferral. Fees and dates for the change are not yet assessed. The signed terms require an agreed written change before extra work begins. Helen has not approved a change.
Language workshop: A second market is a new request, not a free extension
Communication goal: Respond constructively to additional work by distinguishing the agreed scope from the proposed change and explaining options without premature commitments.
Language guidance: A fixed fee applies to the defined scope, not automatically to any additional request. The $60,000 figure is the existing Market A fee. No price or delivery date has yet been established for Market B, and the exercise supplies no basis for inventing one.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand why Market B has become important. / The signed scope covers the six-week Market A study. / A full Market B study is a change to that scope. / The current team cannot complete both full studies by the existing deadline. / We can assess options for resources, timing, and analytical depth. / The revised fee and dates have not yet been assessed. / Additional resources depend on suitable people being available. / More time is one option, subject to agreement. / A high-level scan would have narrower coverage than a full study. / We could schedule Market B as a later phase. / We should show what each option does to Market A. / The terms require an agreed written change before extra work starts. / The request is being assessed, not yet approved. / Agree what the revised deliverable must contain. / Capture the chosen scope, fee, dates, and responsibilities in the variation. / I will return with assessed choices rather than commit to an unsupported deadline.
Vocabulary: scope baseline: The agreed work boundaries against which changes are assessed. / statement of work: A document specifying the work, outputs, and relevant engagement terms. / fixed fee: A specified charge for a defined scope under the agreement. / change request: A proposal to alter agreed work or arrangements. / scope creep: Uncontrolled growth in work beyond the agreed scope. / impact assessment: Examination of a proposed change's consequences. / resource capacity: The work a team can perform with its available people and time. / resource availability: Whether suitable people or other resources can be assigned when needed. / deliverable: A specified output to be supplied. / acceptance criterion: A condition used to determine whether an output meets the agreement. / change authorization: Formal permission for the specified change. / commercial terms: Agreed conditions concerning fees and other business arrangements. / schedule extension: An approved increase in the time allowed. / scope trade-off: Exchanging one work element for another under agreed conditions. / high-level scan: A limited overview rather than a full detailed study. / depth of analysis: The detail and rigor of examination provided. / assumption log: A record of conditions used in planning or analysis. / dependency: A condition or task on which another depends. / rework: Work needed to revise or repeat previous output. / change register: A record of requested changes and their status. / pending approval: Awaiting the relevant authorized decision. / deferral: Moving a decision or activity to a later time. / out-of-scope request: A request beyond the currently agreed work. / written variation: A documented agreed change to specified engagement terms.
Speaking task: Read Helen and Raj aloud, then swap roles. Practice acknowledging urgency before describing the scope and resource effects. Correct all ten gaps. Reread the narrower-scan option and the exchange about starting before authorization. Repeat the options close with timing, Market A effects, acceptance criteria, and the required approval; keep a requested change distinct from agreed work.
Partner: Helen: Client sponsor / Raj: Engagement manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A four-week engagement covers Region East for $35,000. In week two, the client requests Region West as well. Extra work requires an agreed written variation. Its fee, staffing, and timing have not been assessed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Helen: If we choose the narrower scan, the document should say how we will judge whether it has met the agreed brief.
Raj: We will include acceptance criteria for that output. A shared definition of completion helps avoid a later disagreement over whether a preliminary overview was supposed to be a full market study.
Helen: Please bring back the options with their effects on Market A, the timing, and the approval needed for the selected route.
Raj: I will record the request in the change register and return with assessed choices. Once the authorized parties agree the written variation, we can plan the added work against the actual approved terms.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Difficult Client FeedbackThe recommendation depends on staff the client does not have+

Your goal

Acknowledge a missed constraint, restate its effect on the recommendation, and agree a credible correction without becoming defensive or promising an untested solution.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

The recommendation depends on staff the client does not have

Consultant Ronan recommends an eight-week pilot requiring two full-time-equivalent staff throughout. Client operations lead Leila points out that only 0.5 full-time equivalent is available during that period, with no additional hiring approved this quarter. Leila had supplied the limit in a discovery email dated 8 September, which the team missed. The proposed workload has not been redesigned. Ronan must own the oversight, correct the assumption, and assess a smaller scope or different timing. He commits to a revised assessment by Wednesday at 15:00 local time, not to a feasible launch by then.

Leila: Client operations lead / Ronan: Consultant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What staffing did the recommendation assume?

Answer and explanation

A. Two full-time equivalents for eight weeks

The proposed pilot explicitly requires two full-time-equivalent staff throughout the eight-week period.

2. What staffing is actually available?

Answer and explanation

D. 0.5 full-time equivalent during the period

Leila states the available capacity and the absence of approved additional hiring this quarter.

3. What is promised for Wednesday at 15:00?

Answer and explanation

B. A revised assessment

Ronan commits to returning with an assessment, not to solving or authorizing the launch by that time.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Two FTE required and 0.5 FTE available create a 1.5-FTE capacity gap. The available amount is one quarter of the assumed capacity. This does not automatically prove the pilot will take four times as long: tasks, sequencing, and minimum staffing may prevent simple scaling.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
client feedback
Information from the client about the work or its usefulness.respond to client feedback
staffing constraint
A limit on the people or capacity available.acknowledge a staffing constraint
FTE
Full-time equivalent; capacity expressed relative to a defined full-time workload.calculate available FTE
capacity assumption
A planning belief about resources available for the work.correct the capacity assumption
resourcing model
A representation of people, roles, and effort needed.revise the resourcing model
workload
The amount of work assigned or required.assess the workload
feasibility
Whether an option can be carried out under relevant conditions.reassess feasibility
oversight
A missed fact or step; in other contexts, supervisory responsibility.acknowledge the oversight
fact check
Verification of a statement against relevant evidence.complete a fact check
discovery record
Information captured during the initial client inquiry.review the discovery record
assumption register
A documented list of planning assumptions and their status.update the assumption register
capacity gap
A difference between required and available resources.quantify the capacity gap
critical task
Work essential to the intended result or necessary sequence.identify critical tasks
bottleneck
A point limiting the rate or capacity of a process.locate the bottleneck
phasing
Dividing work into sequenced stages.assess phased delivery
scope reduction
A deliberate decrease in included work.evaluate a scope reduction
de-scoping
Removing work from the agreed or proposed scope through the relevant process.make de-scoping explicit
schedule constraint
A limit on when work can occur or finish.clarify schedule constraints
hiring approval
Authorization to recruit or add the relevant staff.confirm hiring approval
client sign-off
The client's relevant formal acceptance or approval.obtain client sign-off
revision note
A record of what changed and why.include a revision note
corrective action
A step addressing an identified problem or error.agree corrective action
accountability
Responsibility for explaining and addressing one's work or decisions.demonstrate accountability
follow-through
Completion of agreed next steps.maintain follow-through

An apology does not by itself repair the recommendation. Correct the documented assumption, identify affected tasks and outputs, and assess alternatives with the client. The Wednesday commitment is to provide that assessment; feasibility and authorization remain separate questions.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge before explaining

When feedback identifies a genuine omission, an immediate defense can make the client repeat the problem more forcefully. State what the team missed and its consequence first. Then clarify the constraint and the repair plan without turning the conversation into a search for someone else to blame.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the miss
You supplied the staffing limit, and we missed it.
State the consequence
Our recommendation depends on capacity that is not available.
Avoid a defensive qualifier
That omission is ours to correct.
Confirm the constraint
The available capacity is half an FTE for this period.
Respect the hiring limit
No additional hiring is approved this quarter.
Quantify the gap
The proposal assumes one and a half FTE more than is available.
Avoid simple scaling
We cannot assume a quarter of the staff means a four-times-longer schedule.
Review the work
We need to identify which tasks and dependencies are affected.
Offer bounded alternatives
We will assess a smaller scope or different timing.
Keep the goal visible
Which parts of the pilot are essential to the decision it must support?
Avoid a promised solution
I cannot yet say which option will be feasible.
Set the next commitment
I will return with the revised assessment by Wednesday at fifteen hundred local time.
Correct the record
We will update the assumption register and mark the previous version as superseded.
Invite factual verification
Please confirm that we have represented the capacity limit correctly.
Separate review from approval
Reviewing an option does not approve its staffing or launch.
Close with follow-through
The next version will state the constraint, its effects, and the assessed alternatives.
6 language and precision notes
We missed it
Owns the documented omission directly.
Depends on
Connects the incorrect assumption to the recommendation.
Not available
Identifies a practical limit, not unwillingness to cooperate.
Cannot assume
Rejects an unsupported arithmetic shortcut.
Will assess
Commits to work whose result is not yet known.
Superseded
Makes clear which version should no longer guide decisions.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening best addresses the feedback?

Answer and explanation

A. You supplied the limit, and we missed it; the recommendation needs revision.

The appropriate response owns the documented omission and connects it to the need for substantive correction.

2. What is the capacity gap?

Answer and explanation

C. 1.5 FTE

Two required FTE minus half an available FTE leaves a gap of one and a half FTE.

3. Why is multiplying the duration by four not a proven fix?

Answer and explanation

C. Task sequencing and minimum staffing may prevent simple proportional scaling.

Capacity ratios describe the gap but do not establish how the actual work can be rescheduled.

4. Which follow-up statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. A revised assessment will be supplied by the agreed time; launch feasibility remains to be established.

The promised deliverable is the revised assessment, not an untested solution or a separate approval.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

The recommendation depends on staff the client does not have

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leila: Client operations lead / Ronan: Consultant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • capacity gap
  • resourcing model
  • revision note
  • phasing
  • capacity assumption
  • feasibility
  • client sign-off
  • scope reduction
  • follow-through
  • staffing constraint
  1. Leila
    This plan still needs two FTEs for eight weeks. My September eighth email said we have half an FTE. How did that constraint disappear from the recommendation?
  2. Ronan
    You did, and we missed that 1. Our recommendation depends on capacity you do not have. That omission is ours to correct, rather than something you should have had to raise again.
  3. Leila
    I need the team to understand that no extra hiring is approved this quarter. We cannot simply add people because the presentation assumes them.
  4. Ronan
    I will correct the 2 explicitly. The current proposal assumes two FTE, while only half an FTE is available during the period. Additional hiring cannot be treated as an agreed solution.
  5. Leila
    That is a large difference. I do not want a cosmetic change to the slide while the same workload remains underneath.
  6. Ronan
    The 3 is one and a half FTE. We need to examine the work itself, not relabel the resource requirement or imply that the original plan fits.
  7. Leila
    One suggestion was to stretch the pilot to four times the duration. That sounds neat on a spreadsheet. Does the work actually divide that way?
  8. Ronan
    That does not establish 4. Some tasks may need people together or depend on a particular sequence. We cannot infer a workable schedule solely by multiplying the duration.
  9. Leila
    What will you check before bringing another option back? The operation still needs a useful answer, but the people limit is real.
  10. Ronan
    We will revisit the 5. Identify the essential tasks, dependencies, and review effort, then assess what could be reduced, phased, or moved without disguising the effect on the pilot's purpose.
  11. Leila
    A smaller pilot might be possible, provided it still tests something meaningful and does not leave the committee with an unusable result.
  12. Ronan
    Any 6 needs a clear consequence statement. We should say which question the smaller test can answer and which part of the original ambition would remain untested.
  13. Leila
    There may also be a later period with different capacity, but I cannot promise those people now.
  14. Ronan
    We can assess that 7 option with its conditions visible. We should not replace one unsupported staffing assumption with another about resources that may become available later.
  15. Leila
    When can I expect a revised assessment? Please distinguish that from telling the committee we have an approved launch plan.
  16. Ronan
    By Wednesday at fifteen hundred local time, I will provide the assessment and a 8. It will state what changed, why it changed, and which feasibility or approval questions remain open.
  17. Leila
    I can check whether the next version accurately represents our capacity. That review will not authorize additional staffing.
  18. Ronan
    Understood. Your factual review is not automatic 9 on a new staffing plan or launch. We will identify the actual approval route for whichever option proves supportable.
  19. Leila
    Then please make sure the previous version is no longer circulated as the current recommendation.
  20. Ronan
    We will mark it superseded and maintain 10. The next version will show the real constraint, the consequences for the work, and the assessed alternatives rather than repeat the original unsupported plan.

Answers and explanations
  1. staffing constraint Turn 2. The half-FTE limit was already supplied; the consultant missed it during discovery.
  2. capacity assumption Turn 4. The plan must reflect half an available FTE and the absence of approved additional hiring.
  3. capacity gap Turn 6. Two required FTE minus half available leaves 1.5 FTE; changing slide wording does not remove it.
  4. feasibility Turn 8. Actual tasks and constraints need examination; a capacity ratio alone does not prove a workable schedule.
  5. resourcing model Turn 10. This maps people and effort by task so the proposal can be rebuilt around real capacity.
  6. scope reduction Turn 12. Removing work changes what the pilot can answer, and that consequence must be explicit.
  7. phasing Turn 14. Later stages require confirmed resources; possible future availability is not a staffing commitment.
  8. revision note Turn 16. The note explains the corrected assumption and changes; Wednesday's promise is an assessment, not launch.
  9. client sign-off Turn 18. Checking factual accuracy is not the same as approving staffing or implementation.
  10. follow-through Turn 20. The repair requires completing the promised revision and version correction, not merely apologizing.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leila: This plan still needs two FTEs for eight weeks. My September eighth email said we have half an FTE. How did that constraint disappear from the recommendation?
  2. Ronan: You did, and we missed that staffing constraint. Our recommendation depends on capacity you do not have. That omission is ours to correct, rather than something you should have had to raise again.
  3. Leila: I need the team to understand that no extra hiring is approved this quarter. We cannot simply add people because the presentation assumes them.
  4. Ronan: I will correct the capacity assumption explicitly. The current proposal assumes two FTE, while only half an FTE is available during the period. Additional hiring cannot be treated as an agreed solution.
  5. Leila: That is a large difference. I do not want a cosmetic change to the slide while the same workload remains underneath.
  6. Ronan: The capacity gap is one and a half FTE. We need to examine the work itself, not relabel the resource requirement or imply that the original plan fits.
  7. Leila: One suggestion was to stretch the pilot to four times the duration. That sounds neat on a spreadsheet. Does the work actually divide that way?
  8. Ronan: That does not establish feasibility. Some tasks may need people together or depend on a particular sequence. We cannot infer a workable schedule solely by multiplying the duration.
  9. Leila: What will you check before bringing another option back? The operation still needs a useful answer, but the people limit is real.
  10. Ronan: We will revisit the resourcing model. Identify the essential tasks, dependencies, and review effort, then assess what could be reduced, phased, or moved without disguising the effect on the pilot's purpose.
  11. Leila: A smaller pilot might be possible, provided it still tests something meaningful and does not leave the committee with an unusable result.
  12. Ronan: Any scope reduction needs a clear consequence statement. We should say which question the smaller test can answer and which part of the original ambition would remain untested.
  13. Leila: There may also be a later period with different capacity, but I cannot promise those people now.
  14. Ronan: We can assess that phasing option with its conditions visible. We should not replace one unsupported staffing assumption with another about resources that may become available later.
  15. Leila: When can I expect a revised assessment? Please distinguish that from telling the committee we have an approved launch plan.
  16. Ronan: By Wednesday at fifteen hundred local time, I will provide the assessment and a revision note. It will state what changed, why it changed, and which feasibility or approval questions remain open.
  17. Leila: I can check whether the next version accurately represents our capacity. That review will not authorize additional staffing.
  18. Ronan: Understood. Your factual review is not automatic client sign-off on a new staffing plan or launch. We will identify the actual approval route for whichever option proves supportable.
  19. Leila: Then please make sure the previous version is no longer circulated as the current recommendation.
  20. Ronan: We will mark it superseded and maintain follow-through. The next version will show the real constraint, the consequences for the work, and the assessed alternatives rather than repeat the original unsupported plan.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The staffing constraint we missed

The lesson's client says the recommendation overlooks a staffing constraint missed during discovery.

  1. Client operations director: Your recommendation ignores our staffing constraint. We raised an operational concern that the discovery work didn't capture.
  2. Engagement lead: You're right that the recommendation doesn't account for it. We need to correct that assumption before presenting implementation as feasible.
  3. Client operations director: I don't want another polished explanation that leaves the actual staffing problem unchanged.
  4. Engagement lead: Understood. Could we confirm the constraint precisely, then reassess how it affects the recommendation?
  5. Client operations director: Yes, but please acknowledge that the current proposal isn't ready simply because the slides are finished.
  6. Engagement lead: Agreed. Completion of the deck doesn't establish implementation readiness or stakeholder buy-in.
  7. Client operations director: Will the next iteration show what changed because of the constraint, rather than just mention it in a footnote?
  8. Engagement lead: Yes. We'll make the revised assumption and its implications visible, while identifying anything that still needs validation.
  9. Client operations director: That would make the correction useful. Let's review the operational requirement before choosing an alternative.
  10. Engagement lead: I'll arrange that review and avoid defending the original recommendation as if its missing assumption were immaterial.
A recommendation sounded like a decision

Separate fictional case: a consulting presentation called a proposed role change the new operating arrangement; the client has not approved it.

  1. Client HR lead: Your presentation made the role change sound decided. Staff are asking when the new arrangement begins.
  2. Consulting manager: Our wording was too definite. It is a recommendation for review, not an approved client decision.
  3. Client HR lead: Please correct that distinction before another team hears the same message from your slides.
  4. Consulting manager: I will. I'll state that approval is pending and remove wording that implies an implementation commitment.
  5. Client HR lead: Does the correction also recognize that we haven't assessed the staffing implications with the affected managers?
  6. Consulting manager: Yes. That is an implementation constraint and a stakeholder discussion still needed, not a completed step.
  7. Client HR lead: I appreciate the correction, but I don't want the team to infer that consultation guarantees acceptance.
  8. Consulting manager: Understood. Consultation informs the decision; it doesn't establish buy-in or predetermine the outcome.
  9. Client HR lead: Let's agree the corrected explanation before it goes into the next readout.
  10. Consulting manager: I'll bring that iteration for review and keep the proposal's status explicit throughout the presentation.
An interview summary omitted the exception

Separate fictional case: a client's interview described a standard approval route and an exception for urgent repairs; the consulting summary included only the standard route.

  1. Client maintenance lead: Your summary left out the urgent-repair exception I described. It makes our process look more rigid than it is.
  2. Consulting analyst: You're right. I summarized the standard route and omitted the exception, which changes the interpretation.
  3. Client maintenance lead: Will you remove the finding that every repair waits for the same approval sequence?
  4. Consulting analyst: I'll correct that unsupported statement and check the exception's scope before revising the analysis.
  5. Client maintenance lead: Please don't swing to the opposite assumption that every urgent repair bypasses all approvals.
  6. Consulting analyst: Agreed. Your explanation establishes an exception exists, not every detail of how it operates.
  7. Client maintenance lead: I can review a corrected process summary with you so the distinction is accurately represented.
  8. Consulting analyst: Thank you. I'll separate the standard route, the stated exception, and any implementation question that remains open.
  9. Client maintenance lead: That will help the next iteration reflect the actual constraint rather than a simplified version.
  10. Consulting analyst: I'll also check which recommendations relied on the incomplete summary and identify the implications of the correction.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The recommendation depends on staff the client does not have
  1. Read Leila and Ronan aloud, then swap roles. Make the acknowledgment of the missed email direct and unqualified.
  2. Check all ten gaps. Reread the staffing arithmetic and explain why it does not establish a four-times-longer schedule.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Correct another capacity assumption

A proposed review requires 1.5 FTE, but the client confirms only 0.5 FTE is available. No extra staffing is approved. The consultant promises a revised assessment on Friday, not a completed review.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Consultant: "The proposal assumed ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 1.5 FTE

One and a half full-time equivalents is the stated requirement in the proposal.

2. Client: "Confirmed available capacity is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 0.5 FTE

Half a full-time equivalent is available, with no extra staffing approved.

3. Consultant: "The resulting capacity gap is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 1.0 FTE

Subtracting half an FTE from one and a half leaves one FTE.

4. Client: "Friday's commitment is a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. revised assessment

The consultant promises an assessment, not completion or authorization of the proposed review.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Consulting English
Professional audience: management consultants, strategy consultants, implementation consultants, analysts, engagement managers, client partners, internal consultants, and advisory teams
Scope: All clients, engagements, people, figures, contracts, policies, and decisions are fictional. This is professional English practice, not legal, financial, statistical, or implementation advice. Real decisions require the applicable evidence, contractual terms, approvals, and qualified review. The cited organizations do not endorse this book.
Lesson 06: Difficult Client Feedback
Original fictional case: Consultant Ronan recommends an eight-week pilot requiring two full-time-equivalent staff throughout. Client operations lead Leila points out that only 0.5 full-time equivalent is available during that period, with no additional hiring approved this quarter. Leila had supplied the limit in a discovery email dated 8 September, which the team missed. The proposed workload has not been redesigned. Ronan must own the oversight, correct the assumption, and assess a smaller scope or different timing. He commits to a revised assessment by Wednesday at 15:00 local time, not to a feasible launch by then.
Language workshop: The recommendation depends on staff the client does not have
Communication goal: Acknowledge a missed constraint, restate its effect on the recommendation, and agree a credible correction without becoming defensive or promising an untested solution.
Language guidance: Two FTE required and 0.5 FTE available create a 1.5-FTE capacity gap. The available amount is one quarter of the assumed capacity. This does not automatically prove the pilot will take four times as long: tasks, sequencing, and minimum staffing may prevent simple scaling.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You supplied the staffing limit, and we missed it. / Our recommendation depends on capacity that is not available. / That omission is ours to correct. / The available capacity is half an FTE for this period. / No additional hiring is approved this quarter. / The proposal assumes one and a half FTE more than is available. / We cannot assume a quarter of the staff means a four-times-longer schedule. / We need to identify which tasks and dependencies are affected. / We will assess a smaller scope or different timing. / Which parts of the pilot are essential to the decision it must support? / I cannot yet say which option will be feasible. / I will return with the revised assessment by Wednesday at fifteen hundred local time. / We will update the assumption register and mark the previous version as superseded. / Please confirm that we have represented the capacity limit correctly. / Reviewing an option does not approve its staffing or launch. / The next version will state the constraint, its effects, and the assessed alternatives.
Vocabulary: client feedback: Information from the client about the work or its usefulness. / staffing constraint: A limit on the people or capacity available. / FTE: Full-time equivalent; capacity expressed relative to a defined full-time workload. / capacity assumption: A planning belief about resources available for the work. / resourcing model: A representation of people, roles, and effort needed. / workload: The amount of work assigned or required. / feasibility: Whether an option can be carried out under relevant conditions. / oversight: A missed fact or step; in other contexts, supervisory responsibility. / fact check: Verification of a statement against relevant evidence. / discovery record: Information captured during the initial client inquiry. / assumption register: A documented list of planning assumptions and their status. / capacity gap: A difference between required and available resources. / critical task: Work essential to the intended result or necessary sequence. / bottleneck: A point limiting the rate or capacity of a process. / phasing: Dividing work into sequenced stages. / scope reduction: A deliberate decrease in included work. / de-scoping: Removing work from the agreed or proposed scope through the relevant process. / schedule constraint: A limit on when work can occur or finish. / hiring approval: Authorization to recruit or add the relevant staff. / client sign-off: The client's relevant formal acceptance or approval. / revision note: A record of what changed and why. / corrective action: A step addressing an identified problem or error. / accountability: Responsibility for explaining and addressing one's work or decisions. / follow-through: Completion of agreed next steps.
Speaking task: Read Leila and Ronan aloud, then swap roles. Make the acknowledgment of the missed email direct and unqualified. Check all ten gaps. Reread the staffing arithmetic and explain why it does not establish a four-times-longer schedule. Repeat the revision commitment with its exact deadline. Say revised assessment, not approved launch plan, and retain the capacity limit.
Partner: Leila: Client operations lead / Ronan: Consultant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A proposed review requires 1.5 FTE, but the client confirms only 0.5 FTE is available. No extra staffing is approved. The consultant promises a revised assessment on Friday, not a completed review.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leila: I can check whether the next version accurately represents our capacity. That review will not authorize additional staffing.
Ronan: Understood. Your factual review is not automatic client sign-off on a new staffing plan or launch. We will identify the actual approval route for whichever option proves supportable.
Leila: Then please make sure the previous version is no longer circulated as the current recommendation.
Ronan: We will mark it superseded and maintain follow-through. The next version will show the real constraint, the consequences for the work, and the assessed alternatives rather than repeat the original unsupported plan.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Ronan: Understood. Your factual review is not automatic client sign-off on a new staffing plan or launch. We will identify the actual approval route for whichever option proves supportable.
Leila: Then please make sure the previous version is no longer circulated as the current recommendation.
Ronan: We will mark it superseded and maintain follow-through. The next version will show the real constraint, the consequences for the work, and the assessed alternatives rather than repeat the original unsupported plan.
END REFERENCE
07Implementation and Change ManagementReceiving the launch email is not readiness+

Your goal

Explain implementation readiness using training, observed practice, operational cover, and ownership rather than treating communication as adoption.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Receiving the launch email is not readiness

A new approval process is scheduled to launch on Monday for 12 managers. All 12 received the announcement; 10 completed the required training, and 4 of those 10 have demonstrated the required tasks in supervised practice. The agreed launch conditions require every participating manager to complete training and demonstrated practice, with operational cover assigned. Eight managers have no verified practice result, two still need training, and cover has not been assigned. Change lead Imani and client process owner David must assess the gaps and seek an authorized revised launch plan, not label unassessed people incompetent.

Imani: Change lead / David: Client process owner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many managers have demonstrated the required tasks?

Answer and explanation

D. Four

Four of the ten trained managers have a verified supervised-practice result.

2. What remains unknown for eight managers?

Answer and explanation

A. Their demonstrated-practice result

Eight lack verified practice results; absence of assessment is not proof of inability.

3. Which launch requirement is entirely unassigned?

Answer and explanation

A. Operational cover

The brief explicitly states that operational cover has not yet been assigned.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

All 12 received the email, 10 completed training, and 4 demonstrated the tasks. These are different measures, not interchangeable adoption rates. Eight have no verified practice result; that is a readiness evidence gap, not evidence that eight people failed an assessment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
change management
Coordinated work helping an organization move to and sustain a different way of operating.plan change management
implementation readiness
Evidence that the required conditions for starting an approach are met.assess implementation readiness
go-live
The point when a process or system begins actual operation.confirm go-live readiness
adoption
Actual use of the intended way of working.measure process adoption
proficiency
Demonstrated ability to perform the relevant tasks to the required level.verify proficiency
training completion
Finishing the specified learning activity.record training completion
supervised practice
Task rehearsal with an appropriate person observing and supporting it.arrange supervised practice
role-based training
Learning tailored to the tasks and responsibilities of a role.provide role-based training
job aid
A concise reference supporting task performance.supply a job aid
dry run
A rehearsal before actual operation.conduct a dry run
practice environment
A setting where tasks can be rehearsed without unintended live effects.use a practice environment
protected time
Time explicitly set aside from competing work for a specified activity.allocate protected time
operational cover
Arrangements maintaining normal work while people undertake another task.assign operational cover
backfill
Replacement capacity for work someone cannot perform during an assignment or absence.arrange backfill
process owner
The person accountable for a defined process and its ongoing management.confirm the process owner
change champion
A person helping colleagues understand and adopt an agreed change.support change champions
readiness gate
A decision point requiring specified launch conditions to be checked.apply the readiness gate
support model
Arrangements for helping users and resolving problems.define the support model
hypercare
A defined period of increased support after launch.plan hypercare
stabilization
Work to bring a new operation into a reliable ongoing state.monitor stabilization
reinforcement
Follow-up support that helps maintain a desired practice.provide reinforcement
usage measure
An indicator of whether and how an approach is being used.define usage measures
benefit realization
Achievement of intended value after a change, under defined measures.track benefit realization
business-as-usual
Normal ongoing operations outside a temporary change effort.maintain business-as-usual service

Training and demonstrated practice are required for each participating manager, and cover is also required. A smaller launch might be assessable, but it is not automatically authorized. Its scope, support, cover, and approval must be explicit before the original plan is replaced.

C Notice the language

Readiness is not a character judgment

People may appear resistant when they lack time, practice, access, or support. Ask which prerequisite is missing before attributing motives. A visible gap can guide support and scheduling; it should not become a public ranking of people whose ability has not been assessed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate communication from readiness
Everyone received the announcement, but the launch conditions are not met.
State the training position
Ten of the twelve managers completed training.
State the practice position
Four have demonstrated the required tasks.
Describe missing evidence fairly
Eight have no verified practice result; that does not mean eight failed.
Name the remaining training
Two managers still need the required training.
Identify the capacity condition
Operational cover has not yet been assigned.
Ask about support
What protected time and practice support do the remaining managers need?
Keep the gate explicit
Each participant must meet the agreed training and practice conditions.
Avoid a blame label
We should not call a missing assessment resistance.
Define a smaller launch
A staged start would need its own approved scope and cover.
Assign ownership
The process owner needs to confirm the operational arrangements.
Prepare assistance
Name the support contact and escalation route before go-live.
Measure actual use
Track the intended workflow after launch, not just attendance at training.
Keep benefits separate
Process use does not by itself prove that the expected benefit has occurred.
Reinforce the change
Review early issues and provide targeted follow-up support.
Close the readiness review
Present the gaps, owners, support needs, and revised decision to the authorized approver.
6 language and precision notes
Different measures
Keeps communication, learning, performance, and use distinct.
No verified result
Describes missing evidence without inventing failure.
Still need
Identifies a practical next step rather than a personal deficiency.
Each participant
Applies the agreed condition to the relevant launch population.
Would need
Makes a staged launch conditional on its own arrangements.
After launch
Locates actual-use measurement in operation, not before it starts.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which readiness statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Four have demonstrated the tasks; eight lack verified practice results.

The statement reports the actual practice evidence without confusing missing results with failure or communication with readiness.

2. Which next step addresses the stated gaps?

Answer and explanation

A. Arrange remaining training, supervised practice, and operational cover before seeking the launch decision.

The stated launch conditions require learning, demonstrated practice, and cover, so each outstanding requirement needs an owned action.

3. What does a usage measure show?

Answer and explanation

D. Actual use of the defined workflow

Usage concerns observed behavior. It does not alone demonstrate independent proficiency, establish a causal service benefit, or prove that every trainee uses the process.

4. How should a staged launch be described?

Answer and explanation

C. A possible revised plan requiring explicit scope, support, cover, and authorization

A smaller start is an option for assessment, not automatic permission to bypass the agreed decision process.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Receiving the launch email is not readiness

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Change lead / David: Client process owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • supervised practice
  • support model
  • implementation readiness
  • operational cover
  • adoption
  • Training completion
  • protected time
  • process owner
  • benefit realization
  • readiness gate
  1. David
    The dashboard is green for Monday because all twelve managers got the announcement. That tells me the email went out. It does not tell me they can run the process.
  2. Imani
    It measures communication, not 1. Ten managers completed training, four demonstrated the required tasks, and operational cover is still unassigned. Those are separate conditions, not details the email resolves.
  3. David
    The four who demonstrated the tasks are among the ten trained managers. The other eight do not have a verified practice result.
  4. Imani
    Then say exactly that about 2. We should not describe eight people as having failed when they have not supplied a verified result. Missing evidence and demonstrated inability are different.
  5. David
    Two managers still need the training itself. Their teams have been busy, and they have not had time set aside.
  6. Imani
    They need 3 and an agreed route to complete the requirement. Calling them resistant would not address the scheduling barrier or tell us whether they can perform the tasks.
  7. David
    Even trained managers need an opportunity to practice. We should not assume that attendance establishes independent performance.
  8. Imani
    Correct. 4 is one condition. The agreed launch gate also requires demonstrated practice for each participating manager, plus cover for their normal responsibilities.
  9. David
    The cover issue worries me. If everyone practices at the same time, the existing approval queue still needs to be handled.
  10. Imani
    We need 5 rather than hope the queue manages itself. Assign the people and timing explicitly, and check the consequences for ongoing service before confirming a launch arrangement.
  11. David
    Would a phased start work? Four have completed the training and practice. Could they begin while we arrange preparation and cover for the other eight?
  12. Imani
    A staged start can be assessed at the 6. It is not automatically approved. We would need a defined scope, cover, support, and the relevant authorization for that revised plan.
  13. David
    I will coordinate those operational arrangements. The change team can support the transition, but somebody in the business must own the process afterward.
  14. Imani
    That is the 7 role. The handover should state who handles exceptions, maintains the instructions, and decides when an issue needs escalation after the project team steps back.
  15. David
    We also need a clear route for questions during the first days. People should not have to find the consultant who last answered an email.
  16. Imani
    Define the 8 before go-live. A job aid, named contact, and clear escalation route can make assistance predictable, but they do not replace the required practice or operational cover.
  17. David
    Once the process starts, we should check whether managers use the intended workflow, rather than keep reporting training attendance as success.
  18. Imani
    Yes, measure 9 in operation. Use the agreed workflow measures and investigate barriers fairly, without assuming every exception means someone is unwilling to change.
  19. David
    And if usage improves, we still need to examine whether the change produces the expected service improvement.
  20. Imani
    That is 10. Keep those measures separate. For now, present the readiness gaps, owners, support needs, and proposed revision to the authorized approver instead of leaving Monday marked ready.

Answers and explanations
  1. implementation readiness Turn 2. Launch requires the stated conditions; receiving the announcement establishes only that communication occurred.
  2. supervised practice Turn 4. An observed task result supports readiness; eight missing results do not mean eight people failed.
  3. protected time Turn 6. Time must be set aside for required learning rather than added silently to full workloads.
  4. Training completion Turn 8. Finishing training is separate from demonstrating the tasks under this agreed readiness plan.
  5. operational cover Turn 10. Normal work needs an assigned arrangement while managers prepare; that requirement remains unresolved.
  6. readiness gate Turn 12. A smaller launch still needs its own scope, cover, support, and authorization.
  7. process owner Turn 14. The owner holds ongoing operational responsibility, distinct from the change team's temporary transition support.
  8. support model Turn 16. Named assistance and escalation arrangements prevent reliance on informal personal contacts after launch.
  9. adoption Turn 18. Adoption means actual workflow use, not pre-launch training attendance or receipt of a notice.
  10. benefit realization Turn 20. The intended business result requires evidence beyond training completion or use of the workflow.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Adoption needs practice, not just an announcement

The lesson's managers are expected to adopt a new process without training or practice time.

  1. Change consultant: Managers are expected to adopt the new process, but they haven't received training or time to practice.
  2. Client transformation lead: We sent the announcement. Shouldn't that be enough for managers to begin following the operating model?
  3. Change consultant: An announcement communicates the change; it doesn't show that people can carry out the new steps.
  4. Client transformation lead: Then the feedback should concern the missing preparation, not accuse managers of resisting adoption.
  5. Change consultant: Exactly. Describe the observed gap, its effect on readiness, and a practical opportunity for supported practice.
  6. Client transformation lead: Could we ask managers to walk through one typical case and identify unclear decision rights?
  7. Change consultant: Yes. That would surface where they need guidance about who can decide or approve an action.
  8. Client transformation lead: I'll arrange the preparation discussion without treating attendance as proof of successful adoption.
  9. Change consultant: Good. Follow-up observation should check how the process is used, not merely whether the message was sent.
  10. Client transformation lead: We'll use that evidence to refine support and distinguish planned change management from demonstrated readiness.
A manager keeps using the old approval route

Separate fictional case: in a practice session, a manager routes a request through the old approval sequence; the new decision-rights guide is available but has not been explained.

  1. Implementation coach: During practice, the request followed the old approval route even though the new guide shows a different sequence.
  2. Client change lead: Should we report that as resistance, or first examine whether the manager understood the revised decision rights?
  3. Implementation coach: Start with the observed behavior. The guide hasn't been explained, so motivation would be an unsupported inference.
  4. Client change lead: I'll ask the manager to describe which step was unclear and what source they were using.
  5. Implementation coach: Good. Then compare the routes using a concrete case and explain who owns each decision.
  6. Client change lead: Could we repeat the practice after the explanation to see whether the new route is clearer?
  7. Implementation coach: Yes. That provides actionable feedback and a chance to demonstrate understanding without claiming permanent adoption.
  8. Client change lead: I'll avoid calling the operating model embedded based on one successful rehearsal.
  9. Implementation coach: Exactly. Practice supports readiness; actual use and continued monitoring answer a different question.
  10. Client change lead: We'll agree that follow-up and use any remaining confusion to improve the implementation support.
A change champion answers every question

Separate fictional case: during a new-process workshop, the change champion answers every case question before the other managers can respond. The coach gives feedback directly between practice rounds.

  1. Implementation coach: You answered each case question before the other managers had a chance to respond in that round.
  2. Change champion: I wanted to keep the session moving. I thought giving the right answer would help everyone learn.
  3. Implementation coach: Your knowledge helps, but that pattern prevents us from seeing whether the other managers can apply the operating model.
  4. Change champion: So a correct answer from me can hide uncertainty about their decision rights?
  5. Implementation coach: Exactly. In the next round, please pause and let the named manager explain their next step first.
  6. Change champion: What should I do if their answer misses an approval responsibility?
  7. Implementation coach: Let them finish. Then ask a clarifying question or offer a correction when invited, using the agreed guide.
  8. Change champion: I will wait for their explanation and support it afterward rather than answer on their behalf.
  9. Implementation coach: Good. I will watch whether more managers demonstrate the process, not just whether we finish quickly.
  10. Change champion: Understood. I will pause in the next round and help clarify after they have responded.

Communication goal: Explain implementation readiness using training, observed practice, operational cover, and ownership rather than treating communication as adoption.
Language guidance: All 12 received the email, 10 completed training, and 4 demonstrated the tasks. These are different measures, not interchangeable adoption rates. Eight have no verified practice result; that is a readiness evidence gap, not evidence that eight people failed an assessment.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Everyone received the announcement, but the launch conditions are not met. / Ten of the twelve managers completed training. / Four have demonstrated the required tasks. / Eight have no verified practice result; that does not mean eight failed. / Two managers still need the required training. / Operational cover has not yet been assigned. / What protected time and practice support do the remaining managers need? / Each participant must meet the agreed training and practice conditions. / We should not call a missing assessment resistance. / A staged start would need its own approved scope and cover. / The process owner needs to confirm the operational arrangements. / Name the support contact and escalation route before go-live. / Track the intended workflow after launch, not just attendance at training. / Process use does not by itself prove that the expected benefit has occurred. / Review early issues and provide targeted follow-up support. / Present the gaps, owners, support needs, and revised decision to the authorized approver.
Vocabulary: change management: Coordinated work helping an organization move to and sustain a different way of operating. / implementation readiness: Evidence that the required conditions for starting an approach are met. / go-live: The point when a process or system begins actual operation. / adoption: Actual use of the intended way of working. / proficiency: Demonstrated ability to perform the relevant tasks to the required level. / training completion: Finishing the specified learning activity. / supervised practice: Task rehearsal with an appropriate person observing and supporting it. / role-based training: Learning tailored to the tasks and responsibilities of a role. / job aid: A concise reference supporting task performance. / dry run: A rehearsal before actual operation. / practice environment: A setting where tasks can be rehearsed without unintended live effects. / protected time: Time explicitly set aside from competing work for a specified activity. / operational cover: Arrangements maintaining normal work while people undertake another task. / backfill: Replacement capacity for work someone cannot perform during an assignment or absence. / process owner: The person accountable for a defined process and its ongoing management. / change champion: A person helping colleagues understand and adopt an agreed change. / readiness gate: A decision point requiring specified launch conditions to be checked. / support model: Arrangements for helping users and resolving problems. / hypercare: A defined period of increased support after launch. / stabilization: Work to bring a new operation into a reliable ongoing state. / reinforcement: Follow-up support that helps maintain a desired practice. / usage measure: An indicator of whether and how an approach is being used. / benefit realization: Achievement of intended value after a change, under defined measures. / business-as-usual: Normal ongoing operations outside a temporary change effort.
Speaking task: Read David and Imani aloud, then swap roles. Keep announcement, training, demonstrated practice, and operational use distinct. Check the ten gaps. Reread the four-of-twelve readiness position and the conditions on a phased start. Repeat the ownership and support close, then state the separate need to measure service benefits after adoption.
Partner: Imani: Change lead / David: Client process owner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Eight supervisors receive a process notice. Six complete training, and three of those six demonstrate the required tasks. Five have no verified practice result. A start still requires assigned cover and approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: David: Once the process starts, we should check whether managers use the intended workflow, rather than keep reporting training attendance as success.
Imani: Yes, measure adoption in operation. Use the agreed workflow measures and investigate barriers fairly, without assuming every exception means someone is unwilling to change.
David: And if usage improves, we still need to examine whether the change produces the expected service improvement.
Imani: That is benefit realization. Keep those measures separate. For now, present the readiness gaps, owners, support needs, and proposed revision to the authorized approver instead of leaving Monday marked ready.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Implementation and Change Management
Original fictional case: A new approval process is scheduled to launch on Monday for 12 managers. 08Steering Committees and Final ReadoutsAgreement on the problem is not approval of an option+

Your goal

Close a divided decision meeting by summarizing trade-offs, distinguishing agreement from authorization, and recording the actual decision and owned follow-ups.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Agreement on the problem is not approval of an option

A steering committee agrees that service handoffs need improvement but is split between two proposals. Option A is estimated at six weeks and $80,000, with manual reporting and staffing still to be confirmed. Option B is estimated at twelve weeks and $140,000, with automated reporting dependent on an unverified system integration. With two minutes left, chair Marta defers the choice to Friday at 10:00 local time. Consultant Ethan will verify the integration position by Thursday at 12:00; operations lead Nora will confirm Option A staffing by the same time. Neither option nor additional implementation spending is approved.

Marta: Steering committee chair / Ethan: Consultant presenting the readout

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the committee agree on?

Answer and explanation

D. The need to improve service handoffs

Agreement concerns the problem, while the option choice and implementation spending remain unapproved.

2. What did Marta decide at the close?

Answer and explanation

B. Defer the option choice to Friday at 10:00 local time

The chair explicitly defers the choice to the stated meeting rather than selecting either option.

3. What must Ethan verify by Thursday at 12:00?

Answer and explanation

D. The system integration position for Option B

Ethan owns the integration follow-up; Nora separately owns Option A staffing confirmation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Option B is estimated to cost $60,000 more and take six weeks longer than Option A. Those differences do not establish that either is preferable. Reporting capability, staffing, integration, and the relative importance of the criteria remain material to the decision.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
steering committee
A governance group overseeing direction and specified decisions.brief the steering committee
readout
A presentation of findings, status, or decisions.deliver the final readout
decision log
A record of choices, authority, conditions, and dates.maintain the decision log
minutes
The formal record of a meeting's relevant discussion and decisions.confirm the minutes
consensus
Shared agreement, whose scope should be stated.establish the scope of consensus
dissent
A stated disagreement with a proposed view or choice.record material dissent
deferred decision
A choice moved to a later specified point.document a deferred decision
conditional approval
Authorization that depends on stated conditions.distinguish conditional approval
endorsement
An expression of support that may differ from formal authorization.clarify the endorsement
decision authority
The role or body permitted to make the relevant choice.confirm decision authority
option appraisal
A structured comparison of alternatives.complete the option appraisal
trade-off
A balance between competing benefits, costs, or conditions.explain the trade-off
implementation estimate
A projection of time or resources needed to execute an option.qualify the implementation estimate
integration dependency
A reliance on connecting systems or processes successfully.verify the integration dependency
manual reporting
Reporting that requires human preparation rather than the proposed automation.account for manual reporting
automated reporting
Reporting produced through defined automated processes.assess automated reporting
decision criterion
A factor used to compare and choose between options.prioritize decision criteria
open item
A question or action not yet resolved.track open items
action owner
The person responsible for a defined next step.name the action owner
due time
The specified time by which a follow-up is required.confirm the due time
decision pack
The material supplied to support a forthcoming choice.update the decision pack
approval status
Whether and on what terms a proposal has been authorized.state the approval status
record correction
An amendment making a decision record accurate.request a record correction
handover
Transfer of relevant responsibility and information.complete the decision handover

Deferral is not conditional approval. Here the committee has authorized neither option nor extra implementation spending. A record saying approved subject to checks would misrepresent the meeting, even though named people have been assigned to resolve the open questions.

C Notice the language

An accurate close can preserve a difficult discussion

A presenter may want to leave a meeting with apparent agreement, especially after extensive work. Do not turn shared concern into a fictional approval. State the unresolved trade-off, the authorized next step, and the decision date so the discussion can advance without rewriting what happened.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the shared ground
We agree that the handoff problem needs to be addressed.
Name the unresolved choice
We have not agreed which option to implement.
Compare the estimates
Option B is estimated at sixty thousand dollars more and six weeks longer.
State the capability difference
Option A uses manual reporting; Option B proposes automated reporting.
Keep the dependency visible
The integration required for Option B has not been verified.
Keep staffing provisional
Option A staffing still needs confirmation.
Respect the close
The chair has deferred the choice to Friday at ten hundred local time.
Avoid fictional approval
This is a deferred decision, not an approval subject to checks.
Assign the technical follow-up
Ethan will verify the integration position by Thursday at noon.
Assign the operational follow-up
Nora will confirm Option A staffing by the same deadline.
Keep spending status clear
No additional implementation spending has been approved.
Preserve disagreement
Record the material trade-offs without claiming unanimous support for an option.
Prepare the next discussion
Update the option comparison with the two follow-up results.
Clarify authority
The committee will make the option decision at the agreed meeting.
Read back the record
May I confirm the decision, owners, due times, and approval status?
Close accurately
The problem is agreed, the option is deferred, and the next steps are assigned.
6 language and precision notes
Shared ground
Separates the agreed problem from the unresolved remedy.
Estimated
Preserves the projected status of cost and schedule figures.
Has not been verified
Keeps a material dependency visible.
Deferred, not approved
Distinguishes two different governance outcomes.
By the same deadline
Aligns follow-ups without merging their owners.
Read back
Gives the chair a chance to correct the record before circulation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which minute accurately records the meeting?

Answer and explanation

D. The option decision is deferred to Friday at 10:00; neither option nor additional spending is approved.

The chair deferred the choice and granted no implementation approval, so the record must preserve that status.

2. What is Option B's estimated cost premium over A?

Answer and explanation

C. $60,000

Subtracting the eighty-thousand estimate from one hundred forty thousand gives a sixty-thousand premium.

3. Who owns Option A staffing confirmation?

Answer and explanation

A. Nora

The brief assigns the staffing follow-up specifically to operations lead Nora.

4. Which closing behavior is most useful?

Answer and explanation

D. Read back the unresolved choice, follow-up owners, deadlines, and approval status.

An explicit read-back preserves what was actually decided and makes the next actions accountable.

Agreement on the problem is not approval of an option

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Marta: Steering committee chair / Ethan: Consultant presenting the readout

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.
